The **scientific method** is an [empirical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_evidence "Empirical evidence") method for acquiring [knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge "Knowledge") through careful [observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observation "Observation"), rigorous [skepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skepticism "Skepticism"), [hypothesis testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis_testing "Hypothesis testing"), and experimental validation. Developed from ancient and medieval practices, it acknowledges that [cognitive assumptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_science#Observation_inseparable_from_theory "Philosophy of science") can distort the interpretation of the [observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perception#Process_and_terminology "Perception"). The scientific method has characterized [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science "Science") since at least the 17th century. Scientific inquiry includes creating a testable [hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis "Hypothesis") through [inductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning "Inductive reasoning"), testing it through experiments and statistical analysis, and adjusting or discarding the hypothesis based on the results.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-principia-1)[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-2)[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-3)
Although procedures vary across [fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branches_of_science "Branches of science"), the underlying [process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Process) is often similar. In more detail: the scientific method involves making [conjectures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjecture "Conjecture") (hypothetical explanations), predicting the logical consequences of hypothesis, then carrying out experiments or empirical observations based on those predictions.[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-NA-4) A hypothesis is a conjecture based on knowledge obtained while seeking answers to the question. Hypotheses can be very specific or broad but must be [falsifiable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiable "Falsifiable"), implying that it is possible to identify a possible outcome of an experiment or observation that conflicts with predictions deduced from the hypothesis; otherwise, the hypothesis cannot be meaningfully tested.[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper1959273-5)
While the scientific method is often presented as a fixed sequence of steps, it actually represents a set of general principles. Not all steps take place in every [scientific inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Scientific_inquiry) (nor to the same degree), and they are not always in the same order.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-allScience-6)[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Inductive_Science_1837-7) Numerous discoveries have not followed the textbook model of the scientific method, and, in some cases, chance has played a role.[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-8)[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-DunbarLuck-9)[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Serendip-10)
## History
Main article: [History of scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_scientific_method "History of scientific method")
For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the history of the scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_history_of_the_scientific_method "Timeline of the history of the scientific method").

The history of the scientific method is different from the [history of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science "History of science") itself. The development of rules for [scientific reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_reasoning "Scientific reasoning") has not been straightforward; the scientific method has been the subject of intense and recurring debate throughout the history of science, and eminent natural philosophers and scientists have argued for the primacy of various approaches to establishing scientific knowledge. 
Different early expressions of [empiricism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empiricism "Empiricism") and the scientific method can be found throughout history, for instance with the ancient [Stoics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoics "Stoics"), [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle "Aristotle"),[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-11) [Epicurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epicurus "Epicurus"),[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Asmis-12) [Alhazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhazen "Alhazen"),[[A]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-vacuum-15)[[a]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-alhacenCharacterizes-16)[[B]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-20)[[i]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Tanqih-21) [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Sina "Ibn Sina"), [Al-Biruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Biruni "Al-Biruni"),[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlikuzai2013154-22)[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTERozhanskayaLevinova1996-23) [Roger Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Bacon "Roger Bacon")[[α]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-26), and [William of Ockham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_of_Ockham "William of Ockham").[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-27)
In the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution "Scientific Revolution") of the 16th and 17th centuries, some of the most important developments were the furthering of [empiricism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empiricism "Empiricism") by [Francis Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Bacon "Francis Bacon") and [Robert Hooke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hooke "Robert Hooke"),[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-28)[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-29) the [rationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalist "Rationalist") approach described by [René Descartes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Descartes "René Descartes"), and [inductivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductivism "Inductivism"), which was further brought to particular prominence by scientists such as [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton "Isaac Newton") and those who followed him. Newton postulated four principles which form the basis of modern science,[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-:12-30) and refined the scientific method.[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-31) Experiments were advocated by Francis Bacon and performed by [Giambattista della Porta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giambattista_della_Porta "Giambattista della Porta"),[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-32) [Johannes Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler "Johannes Kepler"),[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-34)[[d]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Kepler1604-35) and [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei "Galileo Galilei").[[β]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-empirical-37) There was particular development aided by theoretical works by the skeptic [Francisco Sanches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Sanches "Francisco Sanches"),[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanches1988-38) by idealists as well as empiricists [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke "John Locke"), [George Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Berkeley "George Berkeley"), and [David Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hume "David Hume").[[e]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-particDev-41) [C. S. Peirce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._S._Peirce "C. S. Peirce") formulated the [hypothetico-deductive model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothetico-deductive_model "Hypothetico-deductive model") in the 20th century, and the model has undergone significant revision since.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodfrey-Smith2003236-42)
The term _scientific method_ emerged in the 19th century, as a result of significant institutional development of science, and terminologies establishing clear [boundaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demarcation_problem "Demarcation problem") between science and non-science, such as _scientist_ and _pseudoscience_.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThurs2011-43) Throughout the 1830s and 1850s, when Baconianism was popular, naturalists like William Whewell, John Herschel, and John Stuart Mill engaged in debates over "induction" and "facts," and were focused on how to generate knowledge.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThurs2011-43) In the late 19th and early 20th centuries, a debate over [realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_realism "Philosophical realism") vs. [antirealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antirealism "Antirealism") was conducted as powerful scientific theories extended beyond the realm of the observable.[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-auto-44)
### Modern use and critical thought
_scientific method_ came into popular use in the twentieth century; [Dewey's 1910 book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFDewey1910), _[How We Think](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_We_Think "How We Think")_ , inspired [popular guidelines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#aGuideline).[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-cowles-45) It appeared in dictionaries and science textbooks, although there was little consensus on its meaning.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThurs2011-43) Although there was growth through the middle of the twentieth century,[[f]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-deweySchool-46) by the 1960s and 1970s numerous influential philosophers of science such as [Thomas Kuhn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Kuhn "Thomas Kuhn") and [Paul Feyerabend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Feyerabend "Paul Feyerabend") had questioned the universality of the "scientific method", and largely replaced the notion of science as a homogeneous and universal method with that of it being a heterogeneous and local practice.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThurs2011-43) In particular, _[Against Method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Against_Method "Against Method")_ , argued against there being any universal rules of [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science "Science");[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-auto-44) [Karl Popper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Popper "Karl Popper"),[[γ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-48) and Gauch 2003,[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-allScience-6) disagreed with Feyerabend's claim. 
Later stances include physicist [Lee Smolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Smolin "Lee Smolin")'s 2013 essay "There Is No Scientific Method",[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Smolin_2013-49) in which he espouses two [ethical principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#ethicalPosition),[[δ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-ethicalPosition-50) and [historian of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science "History of science") Daniel Thurs' chapter in the 2015 book _Newton's Apple and Other Myths about Science_ , which concluded that the scientific method is a myth or, at best, an idealization.[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Thurs2015-51) As [myths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Beliefs_and_biases) are beliefs,[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-beliefCreatesReality-52) they are subject to the [narrative fallacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative_fallacy "Narrative fallacy"), as pointed out by Taleb.[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-narrativeFallacy-53) Philosophers [Robert Nola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Nola "Robert Nola") and Howard Sankey, in their 2007 book _Theories of Scientific Method_ , said that debates over the scientific method continue, and argued that Feyerabend, despite the title of _Against Method_ , accepted certain rules of method and attempted to justify those rules with a meta methodology.[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-54) Staddon (2017) argues it is a mistake to try following rules in the absence of an algorithmic scientific method; in that case, "science is best understood through examples".[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Staddon_2017_p.-55)[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-56) But algorithmic methods, such as _disproof of existing theory by experiment_ have been used since [Alhacen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhacen "Alhacen") (1027) and his _[Book of Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Optics "Book of Optics")_ ,[[a]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-alhacenCharacterizes-16) and Galileo (1638) and his _Two New Sciences_ ,[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGalileo_Galilei1638-36) and _The Assayer_ ,[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-ilSaggiatore-57) which still stand as scientific method. 
### Overview
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/The_Scientific_Method.svg/250px-The_Scientific_Method.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Scientific_Method.svg)The scientific method is often represented as an [ongoing process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Elements_of_the_scientific_method). This diagram represents one variant, and [there are many others](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Category:Scientific_method "commons:Category:Scientific method").
The scientific method is the process by which [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science "Science") is carried out.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-allTheSciences-58) As in other areas of inquiry, science (through the scientific method) can build on previous knowledge, and unify understanding of its studied topics over time.[[g]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-unification-60) Historically, the development of the scientific method was critical to the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution "Scientific Revolution").[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-lindberg2007-61)
The overall process involves making conjectures ([hypotheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis "Hypothesis")), predicting their logical consequences, then carrying out experiments based on those predictions to determine whether the original conjecture was correct.[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-NA-4) However, there are difficulties in a formulaic statement of method. The scientific method represents general principles rather than a fixed sequence, not all steps occur in every inquiry, nor always in the same order. It requires intelligence, imagination, and creativity rather than rigid adherence to procedure.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGauch20033-62)
#### Factors of scientific inquiry
There are different ways of outlining the basic method used for scientific inquiry. The [scientific community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_community "Scientific community") and [philosophers of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophers_of_science "Philosophers of science") generally agree on the following classification of method components. These methodological elements and organization of procedures tend to be more characteristic of [experimental sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_science "Experimental science") than [social sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_science "Social science"). Nonetheless, the cycle of formulating hypotheses, testing and analyzing the results, and formulating new hypotheses, will resemble the cycle described below.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-63)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Brody-1993-64) It is generally recognized to develop advances in knowledge through the following elements, in varying combinations or contributions:[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Fixation-65)[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Vital-66)
  * Characterizations (observations, definitions, and measurements of the subject of inquiry)
  * Hypotheses (theoretical, hypothetical explanations of observations and measurements of the subject)
  * Predictions (inductive and deductive reasoning from the hypothesis or theory)
  * Experiments (tests of all of the above)


Each element of the scientific method is subject to [peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review "Peer review") for possible mistakes. These activities do not describe all that scientists do but [apply mostly to experimental sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Beliefs_and_biases) (e.g., physics, chemistry, biology, and psychology). The elements above are often taught in [the educational system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education "Education") as "the scientific method".[[C]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-aQuestion-67)
The scientific method is not a single recipe: it requires intelligence, imagination, and creativity.[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-68) In this sense, it is not a mindless set of standards and procedures to follow but is rather an [ongoing cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Evaluation_and_improvement), constantly developing more useful, accurate, and comprehensive models and methods. For example, when Einstein developed the Special and General Theories of Relativity, he did not in any way refute or discount Newton's _Principia_. On the contrary, if the astronomically massive, the feather-light, and the extremely fast are removed from Einstein's theories – all phenomena Newton could not have observed – Newton's equations are what remain. Einstein's theories are expansions and refinements of Newton's theories and, thus, increase confidence in Newton's work. 
[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Brody-1993-64) pragmatic[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-truthSought4sake-19) scheme of the four points above is sometimes offered as a guideline for proceeding:[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-69)
  1. Define a question
  2. Gather information and resources (observe)
  3. Form an explanatory hypothesis
  4. Test the hypothesis by performing an experiment and collecting data in a [reproducible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproducibility "Reproducibility") manner
  5. Analyze the data
  6. Interpret the data and draw conclusions that serve as a starting point for a new hypothesis
  7. Publish results
  8. Retest (frequently done by other scientists)


The iterative cycle inherent in this step-by-step method goes from point 3 to 6 and back to 3 again. 
While this schema outlines a typical hypothesis/testing method,[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGauch2003esp._chapters_5%E2%80%938-70) many philosophers, historians, and sociologists of science, including [Paul Feyerabend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Feyerabend "Paul Feyerabend"),[[h]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-descartes-72) claim that such descriptions of scientific method have little relation to the ways that science is actually practiced. 
The basic elements of the scientific method are illustrated by the following example (which occurred from 1944 to 1953) from the discovery of the structure of DNA (marked with ![DNA label](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/DNA_icon.svg/20px-DNA_icon.svg.png) and indented). 
> ![DNA label](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/DNA_icon.svg/20px-DNA_icon.svg.png) In 1950, it was known that [genetic inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_inheritance "Genetic inheritance") had a mathematical description, starting with the studies of [Gregor Mendel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregor_Mendel "Gregor Mendel"), and that DNA contained genetic information (Oswald Avery's _transforming principle_).[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCarty1985252-73) But the mechanism of storing genetic information (i.e., genes) in DNA was unclear. Researchers in [Bragg's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Lawrence_Bragg "William Lawrence Bragg") laboratory at [Cambridge University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Cambridge "University of Cambridge") made [X-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray "X-ray") [diffraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffraction "Diffraction") pictures of various [molecules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecule "Molecule"), starting with [crystals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal "Crystal") of [salt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt "Salt"), and proceeding to more complicated substances. Using clues painstakingly assembled over decades, beginning with its chemical composition, it was determined that it should be possible to characterize the physical structure of DNA, and the X-ray images would be the vehicle.[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcElheny200434-74)
The scientific method depends upon increasingly sophisticated characterizations of the subjects of investigation. (The _subjects_ can also be called [_unsolved problems_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Lists_of_unsolved_problems "Category:Lists of unsolved problems") or the _unknowns_.)[[C]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-aQuestion-67) For example, [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin") conjectured, correctly, that [St. Elmo's fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Elmo%27s_fire "St. Elmo's fire") was [electrical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical "Electrical") in [nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature "Nature"), but it has taken a long series of experiments and theoretical changes to establish this. While seeking the pertinent properties of the subjects, careful thought may also [entail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logical_consequence "Logical consequence") some definitions and [observations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observations "Observations"); these observations often demand careful [measurements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Measurements "Measurements") and/or counting can take the form of expansive [empirical research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_research "Empirical research"). 
A [scientific question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_question "Research question") can refer to the explanation of a specific [observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observation "Observation"),[[C]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-aQuestion-67) as in "Why is the sky blue?" but can also be open-ended, as in "How can I [design a drug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_design "Drug design") to cure this particular disease?" This stage frequently involves finding and evaluating evidence from previous experiments, personal scientific observations or assertions, as well as the work of other scientists. If the answer is already known, a different question that builds on the evidence can be posed. When applying the scientific method to research, determining a good question can be very difficult and it will affect the outcome of the investigation.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-75)
The systematic, careful collection of measurements or counts of relevant quantities is often the critical difference between [pseudo-sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience "Pseudoscience"), such as alchemy, and science, such as chemistry or biology. Scientific measurements are usually tabulated, graphed, or mapped, and statistical manipulations, such as [correlation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correlation "Correlation") and [regression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regression_analysis "Regression analysis"), performed on them. The measurements might be made in a controlled setting, such as a laboratory, or made on more or less inaccessible or unmanipulatable objects such as stars or human populations. The measurements often require specialized [scientific instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_instrument "Scientific instrument") such as [thermometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermometer "Thermometer"), [spectroscopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectrometer "Spectrometer"), [particle accelerators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_accelerator "Particle accelerator"), or [voltmeters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltmeter "Voltmeter"), and the progress of a scientific field is usually intimately tied to their invention and improvement. 
> I am not accustomed to saying anything with certainty after only one or two observations.
— [Andreas Vesalius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Vesalius "Andreas Vesalius") (1546)[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-76)
#### Definition
The scientific definition of a term sometimes differs substantially from its [natural language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language "Natural language") usage. For example, [mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass "Mass") and [weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weight "Weight") overlap in meaning in common discourse, but have distinct meanings in [mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanics "Mechanics"). Scientific quantities are often characterized by their [units of measure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Units_of_measurement "Units of measurement") which can later be described in terms of conventional physical units when communicating the work. 
New theories are sometimes developed after realizing certain terms have not previously been sufficiently clearly defined. For example, [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein")'s first paper on [relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_relativity "Special relativity") begins by defining [simultaneity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativity_of_simultaneity "Relativity of simultaneity") and the means for determining [length](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Length "Length"). These ideas were skipped over by [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton "Isaac Newton") with, "I do not define [time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_in_physics#Galileo:_the_flow_of_time "Time in physics"), space, place and [motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_\(physics\) "Motion \(physics\)"), as being well known to all." Einstein's paper then demonstrates that they (viz., absolute time and length independent of motion) were approximations. [Francis Crick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Crick "Francis Crick") cautions us that when characterizing a subject, however, it can be premature to define something when it remains ill-understood.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-77) In Crick's study of [consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consciousness "Consciousness"), he actually found it easier to study [awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awareness "Awareness") in the [visual system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_system "Visual system"), rather than to study [free will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_will "Free will"), for example. His cautionary example was the gene; the gene was much more poorly understood before Watson and Crick's pioneering discovery of the structure of DNA; it would have been counterproductive to spend much time on the definition of the gene, before them. 
### Hypothesis development
Main article: [Hypothesis formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis_formation "Hypothesis formation")
> ![DNA label](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/DNA_icon.svg/20px-DNA_icon.svg.png) [Linus Pauling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linus_Pauling "Linus Pauling") proposed that DNA might be a [triple helix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_helix "Triple helix").[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-78)[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-79) This hypothesis was also considered by [Francis Crick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Crick "Francis Crick") and [James D. Watson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_D._Watson "James D. Watson") but discarded. When Watson and Crick learned of Pauling's hypothesis, they understood from existing data that Pauling was wrong.[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-80) and that Pauling would soon admit his difficulties with that structure.
[hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis "Hypothesis") is a suggested explanation of a phenomenon, or alternately a reasoned proposal suggesting a possible correlation between or among a set of phenomena. Normally, hypotheses have the form of a [mathematical model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_model "Mathematical model"). Sometimes, but not always, they can also be formulated as [existential statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existential_quantification "Existential quantification"), stating that some particular instance of the phenomenon being studied has some characteristic and causal explanations, which have the general form of [universal statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_quantification "Universal quantification"), stating that every instance of the phenomenon has a particular characteristic. 
Scientists are free to use whatever resources they have – their own creativity, ideas from other fields, [inductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning "Inductive reasoning"), [Bayesian inference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayesian_inference "Bayesian inference"), and so on – to imagine possible explanations for a phenomenon under study. [[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-81)[[i]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-leapIsInvolved-83) [Charles Sanders Peirce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Sanders_Peirce "Charles Sanders Peirce"), borrowing a page from [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle "Aristotle") (_[Prior Analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prior_Analytics "Prior Analytics")_ , [2.25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquiry#Abduction "Inquiry"))[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-aristotleAbduction-84) described the incipient stages of [inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquiry "Inquiry"), instigated by the "irritation of doubt" to venture a plausible guess, as _[abductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abductive_reasoning "Abductive reasoning")_.[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-How-85): II, p.290  The history of science is filled with stories of scientists claiming a "flash of inspiration", or a hunch, which then motivated them to look for evidence to support or refute their idea. [Michael Polanyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Polanyi "Michael Polanyi") made such creativity the centerpiece of his discussion of methodology. 
[William Glen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Glen_\(geologist_and_historian\) "William Glen \(geologist and historian\)") observes that[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlen199437%E2%80%9338-86)
> the success of a hypothesis, or its service to science, lies not simply in its perceived "truth", or power to displace, subsume or reduce a predecessor idea, but perhaps more in its ability to stimulate the research that will illuminate ... bald suppositions and areas of vagueness.
— William Glen, _The Mass-Extinction Debates_
In general, scientists tend to look for theories that are "[elegant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elegance "Elegance")" or "[beautiful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beauty "Beauty")". Scientists often use these terms to refer to a theory that is following the known facts but is nevertheless relatively simple and easy to handle. [Occam's Razor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occam%27s_Razor "Occam's Razor") serves as a rule of thumb for choosing the most desirable amongst a group of equally explanatory hypotheses. 
To minimize the [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias") that results from entertaining a single hypothesis, [strong inference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strong_inference "Strong inference") emphasizes the need for entertaining multiple alternative hypotheses,[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-platt-87) and avoiding artifacts.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-sn1987a-88)
### Predictions from the hypothesis
Further information: [Prediction § Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prediction#Science "Prediction")
> ![DNA label](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/DNA_icon.svg/20px-DNA_icon.svg.png) [James D. Watson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_D._Watson "James D. Watson"), [Francis Crick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Crick "Francis Crick"), and others hypothesized that DNA had a helical structure. This implied that DNA's X-ray diffraction pattern would be 'x shaped'.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Crick_pp._137%E2%80%93138-89)[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-McElheny_2004_43-90) This prediction followed from the work of Cochran, Crick and Vand[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-HelixTransform-91) (and independently by Stokes). The Cochran-Crick-Vand-Stokes theorem provided a mathematical explanation for the empirical observation that diffraction from helical structures produces x-shaped patterns. In their first paper, Watson and Crick also noted that the [double helix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_helix "Double helix") structure they proposed provided a simple mechanism for [DNA replication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_replication "DNA replication"), writing, "It has not escaped our notice that the specific pairing we have postulated immediately suggests a possible copying mechanism for the genetic material".[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-92)
[predictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prediction "Prediction"), by [reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reasoning "Reasoning") including [deductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning "Deductive reasoning").[[j]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-93) It might predict the outcome of an experiment in a laboratory setting or the observation of a phenomenon in nature. The prediction can also be statistical and deal only with probabilities. 
It is essential that the outcome of testing such a prediction be currently unknown. Only in this case does a successful outcome increase the probability that the hypothesis is true. If the outcome is already known, it is called a consequence and should have already been considered while [formulating the hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Hypothesis_development). 
If the predictions are not accessible by observation or experience, the hypothesis is not yet [testable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testability "Testability") and so will remain to that extent unscientific in a strict sense. A new technology or theory might make the necessary experiments feasible. For example, while a hypothesis on the existence of other intelligent species may be convincing with scientifically based speculation, no known experiment can test this hypothesis. Therefore, science itself can have little to say about the possibility. In the future, a new technique may allow for an experimental test and the speculation would then become part of accepted science. 
For example, Einstein's theory of [general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_relativity "General relativity") makes several specific predictions about the observable structure of [spacetime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spacetime "Spacetime"), such as that [light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light "Light") bends in a [gravitational field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_field "Gravitational field"), and that the amount of bending depends in a precise way on the strength of that gravitational field. [Arthur Eddington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Eddington "Arthur Eddington")'s [observations made during a 1919 solar eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddington_experiment "Eddington experiment") supported General Relativity rather than Newtonian [gravitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitation "Gravitation").[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-94)
### Experiments
Main article: [Experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experiment "Experiment")
> ![DNA label](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/DNA_icon.svg/20px-DNA_icon.svg.png) Watson and Crick showed an initial (and incorrect) proposal for the structure of DNA to a team from [King's College London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King%27s_College_London "King's College London") – [Rosalind Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosalind_Franklin "Rosalind Franklin"), [Maurice Wilkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Wilkins "Maurice Wilkins"), and [Raymond Gosling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Gosling "Raymond Gosling"). Franklin immediately spotted the flaws which concerned the water content. Later Watson saw Franklin's [photo 51](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photo_51 "Photo 51"), a detailed X-ray diffraction image, which showed an X-shape[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-95)[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-photo51Explained-96) and was able to confirm the structure was helical.[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-TeaTime-97)[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Watson_1968_167-98)[[k]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-nextItemToSettle-102)
[crucial experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucial_experiment "Crucial experiment"). If the experimental results confirm the predictions, then the hypotheses are considered more likely to be correct, but might still be wrong and continue to be subject to [further testing.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Evaluation_and_improvement) The [experimental control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_control "Experimental control") is a technique for dealing with observational error. This technique uses the contrast between multiple samples, or observations, or populations, under differing conditions, to see what varies or what remains the same. We vary the conditions for the acts of measurement, to help isolate what has changed. [Mill's canons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill%27s_canons "Mill's canons") can then help us figure out what the important factor is.[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-103) [Factor analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factor_analysis "Factor analysis") is one technique for discovering the important factor in an effect. 
Depending on the predictions, the experiments can have different shapes. It could be a classical experiment in a laboratory setting, a [double-blind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double-blind "Double-blind") study or an archaeological [excavation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excavation_\(archaeology\) "Excavation \(archaeology\)"). Even taking a plane from [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City "New York City") to [Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris "Paris") is an experiment that tests the [aerodynamical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerodynamics "Aerodynamics") hypotheses used for constructing the plane. 
These institutions thereby reduce the research function to a cost/benefit,[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-conjugatePairs-104) which is expressed as money, and the time and attention of the researchers to be expended,[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-conjugatePairs-104) in exchange for a report to their constituents.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-nsf-105) Current large instruments, such as CERN's [Large Hadron Collider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Hadron_Collider "Large Hadron Collider") (LHC),[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-lhc-106) or [LIGO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LIGO "LIGO"),[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-ligo-107) or the [National Ignition Facility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ignition_Facility "National Ignition Facility") (NIF),[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-nif-108) or the [International Space Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Space_Station "International Space Station") (ISS),[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-iss-109) or the [James Webb Space Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Webb_Space_Telescope "James Webb Space Telescope") (JWST),[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-jwst-110)[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-jwstDeploymentSeq-111) entail expected costs of billions of dollars, and timeframes extending over decades. These kinds of institutions affect public policy, on a national or even international basis, and the researchers would require shared access to such machines and their [adjunct infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#otherScientists).[[ε]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-feedTheMachinery-112)[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Crutchfield-113)
[Hipparchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hipparchus "Hipparchus") (190–120 BCE), when determining a value for the precession of the Earth, while [controlled experiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_control "Scientific control") can be seen in the works of [al-Battani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_J%C4%81bir_al-Harr%C4%81n%C4%AB_al-Batt%C4%81n%C4%AB "Muhammad ibn Jābir al-Harrānī al-Battānī") (853–929 CE)[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-114) and [Alhazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#alhazen) (965–1039 CE).[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2001b-115)[[l]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-116)[[b]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-straightLinesOnly-17)
### Communication and iteration
See also: [Scientific literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_literature "Scientific literature") and [Scholarly communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholarly_communication "Scholarly communication")
> ![DNA label](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/DNA_icon.svg/20px-DNA_icon.svg.png) Watson and Crick then produced their model, using this information along with the previously known information about DNA's composition, especially Chargaff's rules of base pairing.[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-SameShape-101) After considerable fruitless experimentation, being discouraged by their superior from continuing, and numerous false starts,[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-118)[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-119)[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-120) Watson and Crick were able to infer the essential structure of [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA "DNA") by concrete [modeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model_\(abstract\) "Model \(abstract\)") [of the physical shapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA#History "DNA") of the [nucleotides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleotide "Nucleotide") which comprise it.[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-SameShape-101)[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-121)[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-122) They were guided by the bond lengths which had been deduced by [Linus Pauling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linus_Pauling "Linus Pauling") and by [Rosalind Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosalind_Franklin "Rosalind Franklin")'s X-ray diffraction images. 
[accuracy and precision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accuracy_and_precision "Accuracy and precision"), so that some consideration will lead the scientist to repeat an earlier part of the process. Failure to develop an interesting hypothesis may lead a scientist to re-define the subject under consideration. Failure of a hypothesis to produce interesting and testable predictions may lead to reconsideration of the hypothesis or of the definition of the subject. Failure of an experiment to produce interesting results may lead a scientist to reconsider the experimental method, the hypothesis, or the definition of the subject. 
[Published papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_publishing#Types_of_academic_paper "Academic publishing") can be built upon. For example: By 1027, [Alhazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhazen "Alhazen"), based on his measurements of the [refraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refraction "Refraction") of light, was able to deduce that [outer space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_space "Outer space") was less dense than [air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air "Air"), that is: "the body of the heavens is rarer than the body of air".[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-alhacenOnRefraction4.28-14) In 1079 [Ibn Mu'adh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Mu%27adh_al-Jayyani "Ibn Mu'adh al-Jayyani")'s _Treatise On Twilight_ was able to infer that Earth's atmosphere was 50 miles thick, based on [atmospheric refraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_refraction "Atmospheric refraction") of the sun's rays.[[m]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-crepusculis-124)
This is why the scientific method is often represented as circular – new information leads to new characterisations, and the cycle of science continues. Measurements collected [can be archived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_data_archiving "Research data archiving"), passed onwards and used by others. [enter the process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#aGuideline) at any stage. They might adopt the characterization and formulate their own hypothesis, or they might adopt the hypothesis and deduce their own predictions. Often the experiment is not done by the person who made the prediction, and the characterization is based on experiments done by someone else. Published results of experiments can also serve as a hypothesis predicting their own reproducibility. 
### Confirmation
Main article: [Reproducibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproducibility "Reproducibility")
Science is a social enterprise, and scientific work tends to be accepted by the scientific community when it has been confirmed. Crucially, experimental and theoretical results must be reproduced by others within the scientific community. Researchers have given their lives for this vision; [Georg Wilhelm Richmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Richmann "Georg Wilhelm Richmann") was killed by [ball lightning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ball_lightning "Ball lightning") (1753) when attempting to replicate the 1752 kite-flying experiment of [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin").[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-125)
[repeated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproducibility "Reproducibility") to produce the same results, this implies that the original results might have been in error. As a result, it is common for a single experiment to be performed multiple times, especially when there are uncontrolled variables or other indications of [experimental error](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observational_error "Observational error"). For significant or surprising results, other scientists may also attempt to replicate the results for themselves, especially if those results would be important to their own work.[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-126) Replication has become a contentious issue in social and biomedical science where treatments are administered to groups of individuals. Typically an _experimental group_ gets the treatment, such as a drug, and the _control group_ gets a placebo. [John Ioannidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ioannidis "John Ioannidis") in 2005 pointed out that the method being used has led to many findings that cannot be replicated.[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-127)
[Peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review "Peer review")—anonymous expert evaluation of research—assesses experimental soundness rather than certifying correctness. Some [journals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_journal "Academic journal") request that the experimenter provide lists of possible peer reviewers, especially if the field is highly specialized. Specialists review methodology and design; if approved (sometimes requiring additional experiments), the prestige of the journal where the work is published indicates perceived quality.[[n]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-128)
Scientists typically are careful in recording their data, a requirement promoted by [Ludwik Fleck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwik_Fleck "Ludwik Fleck") (1896–1961) and others.[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFleck1979xxvii%E2%80%93xxviii-129) Though not typically required, they might be requested to [supply this data](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_sharing "Data sharing") to other scientists who wish to replicate their original results (or parts of their original results), extending to the sharing of any experimental samples that may be difficult to obtain.[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-130) To protect against bad science and fraudulent data, government research-granting agencies such as the [National Science Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Science_Foundation "National Science Foundation"), and science journals, including _Nature_ and _Science_ , have a policy that researchers must archive their data and methods so that other researchers can test the data and methods and build on the research that has gone before. [Scientific data archiving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_data_archiving "Scientific data archiving") can be done at several national archives in the U.S. or the [World Data Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Data_Center "World Data Center"). 
## Foundational principles
See also: [Scientific integrity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_integrity "Scientific integrity") and [Open science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_science "Open science")
The unfettered principles of science are to strive for accuracy and the creed of honesty; openness already being a matter of degrees. Openness is restricted by the general rigour of scepticism. And of course the matter of non-science. 
Smolin, in 2013, espoused ethical principles rather than giving any potentially limited definition of the rules of inquiry.[[δ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-ethicalPosition-50) His ideas stand in the context of the scale of data–driven and [big science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_science "Big science"), which has seen increased importance of honesty and consequently [reproducibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproducibility "Reproducibility"). His thought is that science is a community effort by those who have accreditation and are working within the [community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_community "Scientific community"). He also warns against overzealous parsimony. 
Popper previously took ethical principles even further, going as far as to ascribe value to theories only if they were falsifiable. Popper used the falsifiability criterion to demarcate a scientific theory from a theory like astrology: both "explain" observations, but the scientific theory takes the risk of making predictions that decide whether it is right or wrong:[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Popper0-131)[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Popper1-132)
> "Those among us who are unwilling to expose their ideas to the hazard of refutation do not take part in the game of science." 
— Karl Popper, _The Logic of Scientific Discovery (2002 [1935])_
[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGauch_Jr2002ch._1-133) The nature of [truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth "Truth") and the discussion on how scientific statements relate to reality is best left to the article on the [philosophy of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_science "Philosophy of science") here. More immediately topical limitations show themselves in the observation of reality. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/PositronDiscovery.png/250px-PositronDiscovery.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:PositronDiscovery.png)This [cloud chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloud_chamber "Cloud chamber") photograph is the first observational evidence of [positrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positron "Positron"), 2 August 1932; interpretable only through prior theory.[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Anderson_1933_pp._491%E2%80%93494-134)
It is the natural limitations of scientific inquiry that there is no pure observation as theory is required to interpret empirical data, and observation is therefore influenced by the observer's conceptual framework.[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hanson1958-135) As science is an unfinished project, this does lead to difficulties. Namely, that false conclusions are drawn, because of limited information. 
An example here are the experiments of Kepler and Brahe, used by Hanson to illustrate the concept. Despite observing the same sunrise the two scientists came to different conclusions—their [intersubjectivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersubjectivity "Intersubjectivity") leading to differing conclusions. [Johannes Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler "Johannes Kepler") used [Tycho Brahe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycho_Brahe "Tycho Brahe")'s method of observation, which was to project the image of the Sun on a piece of paper through a pinhole aperture, instead of looking directly at the Sun. He disagreed with Brahe's conclusion that total eclipses of the Sun were impossible because, contrary to Brahe, he knew that there were historical accounts of total eclipses. Instead, he deduced that the images taken would become more accurate, the larger the aperture—this fact is now fundamental for optical system design.[[d]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Kepler1604-35) Another historic example here is the [discovery of Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_Neptune "Discovery of Neptune"), credited as being found via mathematics because previous observers didn't know what they were looking at.[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Lequeux_2021_pp._159%E2%80%93183-136)
Scientific endeavour can be characterised as the pursuit of truths about the natural world or as the elimination of doubt about the same. The former is the direct construction of explanations from empirical data and logic, the latter the reduction of potential explanations.[[ζ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-138) It was established [above](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#th-v-obs) how the interpretation of empirical data is theory-laden, so neither approach is trivial. 
The ubiquitous element in the scientific method is [empiricism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empiricism "Empiricism"), which holds that knowledge is created by a process involving observation; scientific theories generalize observations. This is in opposition to stringent forms of [rationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalism "Rationalism"), which holds that knowledge is created by the human intellect; later clarified by Popper to be built on prior theory.[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodfrey-Smith200319%E2%80%9374-139) The scientific method embodies the position that reason alone cannot solve a particular scientific problem; it unequivocally refutes claims that [revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation "Revelation"), political or religious [dogma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogma "Dogma"), appeals to tradition, commonly held beliefs, common sense, or currently held theories pose the only possible means of demonstrating truth.[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-truthSought4sake-19)[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-reasonsFirstRule-100)
In 1877,[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Fixation-65) [C. S. Peirce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._S._Peirce "C. S. Peirce") characterized inquiry in general not as the pursuit of truth _per se_ but as the struggle to move from irritating, inhibitory doubts born of surprises, disagreements, and the like, and to reach a secure belief, the belief being that on which one is prepared to act. His [pragmatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pragmatism "Pragmatism") views framed scientific inquiry as part of a broader spectrum and as spurred, like inquiry generally, by actual doubt, not mere verbal or "hyperbolic doubt", which he held to be fruitless.[[o]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-142) This "hyperbolic doubt" Peirce argues against here is of course just another name for [Cartesian doubt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartesian_doubt "Cartesian doubt") associated with [René Descartes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Descartes "René Descartes"). It is a methodological route to certain knowledge by identifying what can't be doubted. 
A strong formulation of the scientific method is not always aligned with a form of [empiricism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empiricism "Empiricism") in which the empirical data is put forward in the form of experience or other abstracted forms of knowledge as in current scientific practice the use of [scientific modelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_modelling "Scientific modelling") and reliance on abstract typologies and theories is normally accepted. In 2010, [Hawking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Hawking "Stephen Hawking") suggested that physics' models of reality should simply be accepted where they prove to make useful predictions. He calls the concept [model-dependent realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model-dependent_realism "Model-dependent realism").[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hawking-143)
## Rationality
The following section will first explore beliefs and biases, and then get to the rational reasoning most associated with the sciences.[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGauch_Jr200229%E2%80%9331-144)
### Beliefs and biases
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Jean_Louis_Th%C3%A9odore_G%C3%A9ricault_001.jpg/250px-Jean_Louis_Th%C3%A9odore_G%C3%A9ricault_001.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jean_Louis_Th%C3%A9odore_G%C3%A9ricault_001.jpg)
Flying gallop as shown by this painting ([Théodore Géricault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9odore_G%C3%A9ricault "Théodore Géricault"), 1821) is [falsified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability "Falsifiability"); see below.
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/The_Horse_in_Motion_high_res.jpg/250px-The_Horse_in_Motion_high_res.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Horse_in_Motion_high_res.jpg)
[Muybridge's photographs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sallie_Gardner_at_a_Gallop "Sallie Gardner at a Gallop") of _The Horse in Motion_ , 1878, were used to answer the question of whether all four feet of a galloping horse are ever off the ground at the same time. This demonstrates a use of photography as an experimental tool in science.
Scientific methodology often directs that [hypotheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothesis "Hypothesis") be tested in [controlled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_control "Scientific control") conditions wherever possible. This is frequently possible in certain areas, such as in the biological sciences, and more difficult in other areas, such as in astronomy. 
The practice of experimental control and reproducibility can have the effect of diminishing the potentially harmful effects of circumstance, and to a degree, personal bias. For example, pre-existing beliefs can alter the interpretation of results, as in [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias"); this is a [heuristic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuristic "Heuristic") that leads a person with a particular belief to see things as reinforcing their belief, even if another observer might disagree (in other words, people tend to observe what they expect to observe).[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-beliefCreatesReality-52)
> [T]he action of thought is excited by the irritation of doubt, and ceases when belief is attained.
— [C.S. Peirce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C.S._Peirce "C.S. Peirce"), _How to Make Our Ideas Clear_ (1877)[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-How-85)
A historical example is the belief that the legs of a [galloping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_gallop "Horse gallop") horse are splayed at the point when none of the horse's legs touch the ground, to the point of this image being included in paintings by its supporters. However, the first stop-action pictures of a horse's gallop by [Eadweard Muybridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eadweard_Muybridge "Eadweard Muybridge") showed this to be false, and that the legs are instead gathered together.[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-145)
Another important human bias that plays a role is a preference for new, surprising statements (see _[Appeal to novelty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeal_to_novelty "Appeal to novelty")_), which can result in a search for evidence that the new is true.[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldhaberNieto2010940-146) Poorly attested beliefs can be believed and acted upon via a less rigorous heuristic.[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-mythIsAbelief-147)
[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldhaberNieto2010942-148) When a narrative is constructed its elements become easier to believe.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos19761%E2%80%9319-149)[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-narrativeFallacy-53)
[Fleck (1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFFleck1979), p. 27 notes "Words and ideas are originally phonetic and mental equivalences of the experiences coinciding with them. ... Such proto-ideas are at first always too broad and insufficiently specialized. ... Once a structurally complete and closed system of opinions consisting of many details and relations has been formed, it offers enduring resistance to anything that contradicts it". Sometimes, these relations have their elements assumed _[a priori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_priori_and_a_posteriori "A priori and a posteriori")_ , or contain some other logical or methodological flaw in the process that ultimately produced them. [Donald M. MacKay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_M._MacKay "Donald M. MacKay") has analyzed these elements in terms of limits to the accuracy of measurement and has related them to instrumental elements in a category of measurement.[[η]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-macKay-150)
### Deductive and inductive reasoning
Main articles: [Deductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning "Deductive reasoning") and [Inductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning "Inductive reasoning")
The idea of there being two opposed justifications for truth has shown up throughout the history of scientific method as analysis versus synthesis, non-ampliative/ampliative, or even confirmation and verification. (And there are other kinds of reasoning.) One to use what is observed to build towards fundamental truths – and the other to derive from those fundamental truths more specific principles.[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-SEP_SM-151)
Deductive reasoning derives specific conclusions from established general principles—if the premises are true, the conclusion must be true. Inductive reasoning builds general principles from observations—conclusions are probable but not guaranteed. Scientific inquiry employs both: induction generates hypotheses from observations; deduction predicts testable consequences. This process requires stringent scepticism regarding observed phenomena, because cognitive assumptions can distort the interpretation of initial perceptions.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Gauch_Jr_2002_p30/ch4-152)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/Perihelio.svg/250px-Perihelio.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Perihelio.svg)[Precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apsidal_precession "Apsidal precession") of the [perihelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelion_and_aphelion "Perihelion and aphelion") – exaggerated in the case of Mercury, but observed in the case of [S2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2_\(star\) "S2 \(star\)")'s [apsidal precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apsidal_precession "Apsidal precession") around [Sagittarius A*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_A* "Sagittarius A*")[[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-153) [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Inductive_Deductive_Reasoning.svg/250px-Inductive_Deductive_Reasoning.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Inductive_Deductive_Reasoning.svg)Inductive Deductive Reasoning
[history of gravitational theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_gravitational_theory "History of gravitational theory").[[p]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-155) It took thousands of years of measurements, from the [Chaldean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaldea "Chaldea"), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India"), [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Iran "History of Iran"), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greece "Greece"), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs "Arabs"), and [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe "Ethnic groups in Europe") astronomers, to fully record the motion of planet [Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth "Earth").[[q]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Astronomy101-157) [Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler "Johannes Kepler")(and others) were then able to build their early theories by [generalizing the collected data inductively](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning#inductive_generalization "Inductive reasoning"), and [Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton "Isaac Newton") was able to unify prior theory and measurements into the consequences of his [laws of motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton%27s_laws_of_motion "Newton's laws of motion") in 1727.[[r]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-keplerNewton-159)
Another common example of inductive reasoning is the observation of a [counterexample](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterexample "Counterexample") to current theory inducing the need for new ideas. [Le Verrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbain_Le_Verrier "Urbain Le Verrier") in 1859 pointed out problems with the [perihelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelion_and_aphelion "Perihelion and aphelion") of [Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(planet\) "Mercury \(planet\)") that showed Newton's theory to be at least incomplete. The observed difference of Mercury's [precession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apsidal_precession "Apsidal precession") between Newtonian theory and observation was one of the things that occurred to [Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein") as a possible early test of his [theory of relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_relativity "Theory of relativity"). His relativistic calculations matched observation much more closely than Newtonian theory did.[[s]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-LeVerrier1859-160) Though, today's [Standard Model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Model "Standard Model") of physics suggests that we still do not know at least some of the concepts surrounding Einstein's theory, it holds to this day and is being built on deductively. 
A theory being assumed as true and subsequently built on is a common example of deductive reasoning. Theory building on Einstein's achievement can simply state that 'we have shown that this case fulfils the conditions under which general/special relativity applies, therefore its conclusions apply also'. If it was properly shown that 'this case' fulfils the conditions, the conclusion follows. An extension of this is the assumption of a solution to an open problem. This weaker kind of deductive reasoning will get used in current research, when multiple scientists or even teams of researchers are all gradually solving specific cases in working towards proving a larger theory. This often sees hypotheses being revised again and again as new proof emerges. 
This way of presenting inductive and deductive reasoning shows part of why science is often presented as being a cycle of iteration. It is important to keep in mind that that cycle's foundations lie in reasoning, and not wholly in the following of procedure. 
Claims of scientific truth can be opposed in three ways: by falsifying them, by questioning their certainty, or by asserting the claim itself to be incoherent.[[t]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-161) Incoherence, here, means internal errors in logic, like stating opposites to be true; falsification is what Popper would have called the honest work of conjecture and refutation[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-trialAndErr-47) — certainty, perhaps, is where difficulties in telling truths from non-truths arise most easily. 
Scientific measurements include [uncertainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty "Uncertainty") estimates, calculated through repeated measurements, error propagation from underlying quantities, or sampling limitations. uncertainty.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-conjugatePairs-104) Counts of things may represent a sample of desired quantities, with an uncertainty that depends upon the [sampling method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampling_method "Sampling method") used and the number of samples taken. 
In the case of measurement imprecision, there will simply be a 'probable deviation' expressing itself in a study's conclusions. Statistics are different. [Inductive statistical generalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning#Statistical_generalisation "Inductive reasoning") will take sample data and extrapolate more general conclusions, which has to be justified — and scrutinised. It can even be said that statistical models are only ever useful, [but never a complete representation of circumstances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_models_are_wrong "All models are wrong"). 
In statistical analysis, expected and unexpected bias is a large factor.[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Welsby_Weatherall_2022_pp._793%E2%80%93798-162) [Research questions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_question "Research question"), the collection of data, or the interpretation of results, all are subject to larger amounts of scrutiny than in comfortably logical environments. Statistical models go through a [process for validation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_model_validation "Statistical model validation"), for which one could even say that awareness of potential biases is more important than the hard logic; errors in logic are easier to find in [peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review "Peer review"), after all.[[u]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-164) More general, claims to rational knowledge, and especially statistics, have to be put into their appropriate context.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Gauch_Jr_2002_p30/ch4-152) Simple statements such as '9 out of 10 doctors recommend' are therefore of unknown quality because they do not justify their methodology. 
Lack of familiarity with statistical methodologies can result in erroneous conclusions. Foregoing the easy example,[[v]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-165) multiple probabilities interacting is where, for example medical professionals,[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Gigerenzer_2015-166) have shown a lack of proper understanding. [Bayes' theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayes%27_theorem "Bayes' theorem") is the mathematical principle lining out how standing probabilities are adjusted given new information. The [boy or girl paradox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_or_girl_paradox "Boy or girl paradox") is a common example. In knowledge representation, [Bayesian estimation of mutual information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_information#Bayesian_estimation_of_mutual_information "Mutual information") between [random variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Random_variable "Random variable") is a way to measure dependence, independence, or interdependence of the information under scrutiny.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-prml-167)
Beyond commonly associated [survey methodology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survey_methodology "Survey methodology") of [field research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_research "Field research"), the concept together with [probabilistic reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probabilistic_reasoning "Probabilistic reasoning") is used to advance fields of science where research objects have no definitive states of being. For example, in [statistical mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_mechanics "Statistical mechanics"). 
## Methods of inquiry
### Hypothetico-deductive method
The [hypothetico-deductive model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothetico-deductive_model "Hypothetico-deductive model"), or hypothesis-testing method, or "traditional" scientific method is, as the name implies, based on the formation of [hypotheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypotheses "Hypotheses") and their testing via [deductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning "Deductive reasoning"). A hypothesis stating implications, often called [predictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prediction "Prediction"), that are falsifiable via experiment is of central importance here, as not the hypothesis but its implications are what is tested.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168) Basically, scientists will look at the hypothetical consequences a (potential) [theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory "Theory") holds and prove or disprove those instead of the theory itself. If an [experimental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experiment "Experiment") test of those hypothetical consequences shows them to be false, it follows logically that the part of the theory that implied them was false also. If they show as true however, it does not prove the theory definitively. 
The [logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic "Logic") of this testing is what affords this method of inquiry to be reasoned deductively. The formulated hypothesis is assumed to be 'true', and from that 'true' statement implications are inferred. If the following tests show the implications to be false, it follows that the hypothesis was false also. If test show the implications to be true, new insights will be gained. It is important to be aware that a positive test here will at best strongly imply but not definitively prove the tested hypothesis, as deductive inference (A ⇒ B) is not equivalent like that; only (¬B ⇒ ¬A) is valid logic. Their positive outcomes however, as Hempel put it, provide "at least some support, some corroboration or confirmation for it".[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hempel_1966-169) This is why [Popper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Popper "Frank Popper") insisted on fielded hypotheses to be falsifieable, as successful tests imply very little otherwise. As [Gillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_A._Gillies "Donald A. Gillies") put it, "successful theories are those that survive elimination through falsification".[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168)
Deductive reasoning in this mode of inquiry will sometimes be replaced by [abductive reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abductive_reasoning "Abductive reasoning")—the search for the most plausible explanation via logical inference.
