For example, in biology, where general laws are few,[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168) as valid deductions rely on solid presuppositions.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Gauch_Jr_2002_p30/ch4-152)
### Inductive method
The [inductivist approach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductivism "Inductivism") to deriving scientific truth first rose to prominence with [Francis Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Bacon "Francis Bacon") and particularly with [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton "Isaac Newton") and those who followed him.[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-novOrganon-170) After the establishment of the [HD-method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothetico-deductive_model "Hypothetico-deductive model"), it was often put aside as something of a "fishing expedition" though.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168) It is still valid to some degree, but today's inductive method is often far removed from the historic approach—the scale of the data collected lending new effectiveness to the method. It is most-associated with data-mining projects or large-scale observation projects. In both these cases, it is often not at all clear what the results of proposed experiments will be, and thus knowledge will arise after the collection of data through inductive reasoning.[[r]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-keplerNewton-159)
Where the traditional method of inquiry does both, the inductive approach usually formulates only a [research question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_question "Research question"), not a hypothesis. Following the initial question instead, a suitable "high-throughput method" of data-collection is determined, the resulting data processed and 'cleaned up', and conclusions drawn after. "This shift in focus elevates the data to the supreme role of revealing novel insights by themselves".[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168)
The advantage the inductive method has over methods formulating a hypothesis that it is essentially free of "a researcher's preconceived notions" regarding their subject. On the other hand, inductive reasoning is always attached to a measure of certainty, as all inductively reasoned conclusions are.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168) This measure of certainty can reach quite high degrees, though. For example, in the determination of large [primes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_number "Prime number"), which are used in [encryption software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encryption_software "Encryption software").[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGauch2003159-171)
### Mathematical modelling
[Mathematical modelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_modelling "Mathematical modelling"), or allochthonous reasoning, typically is the formulation of a hypothesis followed by building mathematical constructs that can be tested in place of conducting physical laboratory experiments. This approach has two main factors: simplification/abstraction and secondly a set of correspondence rules. The correspondence rules lay out how the constructed model will relate back to reality-how truth is derived; and the simplifying steps taken in the abstraction of the given system are to reduce factors that do not bear relevance and thereby reduce unexpected errors.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168) These steps can also help the researcher in understanding the important factors of the system, how far parsimony can be taken until the system becomes more and more unchangeable and thereby stable. Parsimony and related principles are further explored [below](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Confirmation_theory). 
Once this translation into mathematics is complete, the resulting model, in place of the corresponding system, can be analysed through purely mathematical and computational means. The results of this analysis are of course also purely mathematical in nature and get translated back to the system as it exists in reality via the previously determined correspondence rules—iteration following review and interpretation of the findings. The way such models are reasoned will often be mathematically deductive—but they don't have to be. An example here are [Monte-Carlo simulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Carlo_method "Monte Carlo method"). These generate empirical data "arbitrarily", and, while they may not be able to reveal universal principles, they can nevertheless be useful.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVoit2019-168)
## Scientific inquiry
Scientific inquiry generally aims to obtain [knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge "Knowledge") in the form of [testable explanations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#suitableTest)[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-SuitableTest-172)[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-econ-99) that scientists can use to [predict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predictability "Predictability") the results of future experiments. This allows scientists to gain a better understanding of the topic under study, and later to use that understanding to intervene in its causal mechanisms (such as to cure disease). The better an explanation is at making predictions, the more useful it frequently can be, and the more likely it will continue to explain a body of evidence better than its alternatives. The most successful explanations – those that explain and make accurate predictions in a wide range of circumstances – are often called [scientific theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_theories "Scientific theories").[[C]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-aQuestion-67)
Most experimental results do not produce large changes in human understanding; improvements in theoretical scientific understanding typically result from a gradual process of development over time, sometimes across different domains of science.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-173) Scientific models vary in the extent to which they have been experimentally tested and for how long, and in their acceptance in the scientific community. In general, explanations become accepted over time as evidence accumulates on a given topic, and the explanation in question proves more powerful than its alternatives at explaining the evidence. Often subsequent researchers re-formulate the explanations over time, or combined explanations to produce new explanations. 
### Properties of scientific inquiry
Scientific knowledge is closely tied to [empirical findings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_evidence "Empirical evidence") and can remain subject to [falsification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability "Falsifiability") if new experimental observations are incompatible with what is found. That is, no theory can ever be considered final since new problematic evidence might be discovered. If such evidence is found, a new theory may be proposed, or (more commonly) it is found that modifications to the previous theory are sufficient to explain the new evidence. The strength of a theory relates to how long it has persisted without major alteration to its core principles. 
Theories can also become subsumed by other theories. For example, Newton's laws explained thousands of years of scientific observations of the planets [almost perfectly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#precession_of_Mercury). However, these laws were then determined to be special cases of a more general theory ([relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_relativity "Theory of relativity")), which explained both the (previously unexplained) exceptions to Newton's laws and predicted and explained other observations such as the deflection of [light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light "Light") by [gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity "Gravity"). Thus, in certain cases independent, unconnected, scientific observations can be connected, unified by principles of increasing explanatory power.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrody199344%E2%80%9345-174)[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldhaberNieto2010942-148)
Since new theories might be more comprehensive than what preceded them, and thus be able to explain more than previous ones, successor theories might be able to meet a higher standard by explaining a larger body of observations than their predecessors.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrody199344%E2%80%9345-174) For example, the theory of [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution "Evolution") explains the [diversity of life on Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity "Biodiversity"), how species adapt to their environments, and many other [patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pattern "Pattern") observed in the natural world;[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hall08-175)[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Cracraft05-176) its most recent major modification was unification with [genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics "Genetics") to form the [modern evolutionary synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_evolutionary_synthesis "Extended evolutionary synthesis"). In subsequent modifications, it has also subsumed aspects of many other fields such as [biochemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biochemistry "Biochemistry") and [molecular biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_biology "Molecular biology"). 
## Heuristics
### Confirmation theory
During the course of history, one theory has succeeded another, and some have suggested further work while others have seemed content just to explain the phenomena. The reasons why one theory has replaced another are not always obvious or simple. The philosophy of science includes the question: _What criteria are satisfied by a 'good' theory_. This question has a long history, and many scientists, as well as philosophers, have considered it. The objective is to be able to choose one theory as preferable to another without introducing [cognitive bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_bias "Cognitive bias").[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Kuhn-177) Though different thinkers emphasize different aspects,[[ι]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-183) good theories are accurate, internally consistent, explanatory beyond required data, unifying of disparate phenomena, and fruitful for research. When empirical evidence is limited, scientists favor [parsimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occum%27s_razor "Occum's razor") and invariant observations. Scientists will sometimes also list the very subjective criteria of "formal elegance" which can indicate multiple different things. 
The goal here is to make the choice between theories less arbitrary. Nonetheless, these criteria contain subjective elements, and should be considered [heuristics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuristics "Heuristics") rather than a definitive.[[κ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Pars_and_El-185) Also, criteria such as these do not necessarily decide between alternative theories. Quoting [Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Bird "Alexander Bird"):[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Bird-186)
> "[Such criteria] cannot determine scientific choice. First, which features of a theory satisfy these criteria may be disputable (_e.g._ does simplicity concern the ontological commitments of a theory or its mathematical form?). Secondly, these criteria are imprecise, and so there is room for disagreement about the degree to which they hold. Thirdly, there can be disagreement about how they are to be weighted relative to one another, especially when they conflict."
It also is debatable whether existing scientific theories satisfy all these criteria, which may represent goals not yet achieved. For example, explanatory power over all existing observations is satisfied by no one theory at the moment.[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Realism-187)[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Davies-188)
#### Parsimony
The [desiderata](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/desiderata "wikt:desiderata") of a "good" theory have been debated for centuries, going back perhaps even earlier than [Occam's razor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occam%27s_razor "Occam's razor"),[[w]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-189) which is often taken as an attribute of a good theory. Science tries to be simple. When gathered data supports multiple explanations, the most simple explanation for phenomena or the most simple formation of a theory is recommended by the principle of parsimony.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGauch2003269-190) Scientists go as far as to call simple proofs of complex statements _beautiful_. 
> We are to admit no more causes of natural things than such as are both true and sufficient to explain their appearances.
— Isaac Newton, _Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica (1723 [3rd ed.])_[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-principia-1)
The concept of parsimony should not be held to imply complete frugality in the pursuit of scientific truth. The general process starts at the opposite end of there being a vast number of potential explanations and general disorder. An example can be seen in [Paul Krugman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Krugman "Paul Krugman")'s process, who makes explicit to "dare to be silly". He writes that in his work on new theories of international trade he [reviewed prior work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_review "Literature review") with an open frame of mind and broadened his initial viewpoint even in unlikely directions. Once he had a sufficient body of ideas, he would try to simplify and thus find what worked among what did not. Specific to Krugman here was to "question the question". He recognised that prior work had applied erroneous models to already present evidence, commenting that "intelligent commentary was ignored".[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Krugman-191) Thus touching on the need to bridge the common bias against other circles of thought.[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFleck197927-192)
#### Elegance
Occam's razor might fall under the heading of "simple elegance", but it is arguable that _parsimony_ and _elegance_ pull in different directions. Introducing additional elements could simplify theory formulation, whereas simplifying a theory's ontology might lead to increased syntactical complexity.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Baker-184)
Sometimes ad-hoc modifications of a failing idea may also be dismissed as lacking "formal elegance". This appeal to what may be called "aesthetic" is hard to characterise, but essentially about a sort of familiarity. Though, argument based on "elegance" is contentious and over-reliance on familiarity will breed stagnation.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Colyvan-179)
#### Invariance
Principles of invariance have been a theme in scientific writing, and especially physics, since at least the early 20th century.[[θ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-invariantTheme-182) The basic idea here is that good structures to look for are those independent of perspective, an idea that has featured earlier of course for example in [Mill's Methods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill%27s_Methods "Mill's Methods") of difference and agreement—methods that would be referred back to in the context of contrast and invariance.[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-O&H_1995-193) But as tends to be the case, there is a difference between something being a basic consideration and something being given weight. Principles of invariance have only been given weight in the wake of Einstein's theories of relativity, which reduced everything to relations and were thereby fundamentally unchangeable, unable to be varied.[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Wigner1967-194)[[x]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-WeinertEddingtonEinstein-195) As [David Deutsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Deutsch "David Deutsch") put it in 2009: "the search for hard-to-vary explanations is the origin of all progress".[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-DD_TED-181)
An example here can be found in one of [Einstein's thought experiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein%27s_thought_experiments#Falling_painters_and_accelerating_elevators "Einstein's thought experiments"). The one of a lab suspended in empty space is an example of a useful invariant observation. He imagined the absence of gravity and an experimenter free floating in the lab. — If now an entity pulls the lab upwards, accelerating uniformly, the experimenter would perceive the resulting force as gravity. The entity however would feel the work needed to accelerate the lab continuously.[[x]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-WeinertEddingtonEinstein-195) Through this experiment Einstein was able to equate gravitational and inertial mass; something unexplained by Newton's laws, and an early but "powerful argument for a generalised postulate of relativity".[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Einstein1916-196)
> The feature, which suggests reality, is always some kind of invariance of a structure independent of the aspect, the projection.
— [Max Born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Born "Max Born"), 'Physical Reality' (1953), 149 — as quoted by Weinert (2004)[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Weinert_2004-180)
The discussion on [invariance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invariance_\(physics\) "Invariance \(physics\)") in physics is often had in the more specific context of [symmetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symmetry_\(physics\) "Symmetry \(physics\)").[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Wigner1967-194) The Einstein example above, in the parlance of Mill would be an agreement between two values. In the context of invariance, it is a variable that remains unchanged through some kind of transformation or change in perspective. And discussion focused on symmetry would view the two perspectives as systems that share a relevant aspect and are therefore symmetrical. 
Related principles here are [falsifiability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability "Falsifiability") and [testability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testability "Testability"). The opposite of something being _hard-to-vary_ are theories that resist falsification—a frustration that was expressed colourfully by [Wolfgang Pauli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Pauli "Wolfgang Pauli") as them being "[not even wrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Not_even_wrong "Not even wrong")". The importance of scientific theories to be falsifiable finds especial emphasis in the philosophy of Karl Popper. The broader view here is testability, since it includes the former and allows for additional practical considerations.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-197)[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-l921-198)
## Philosophy and discourse
See also: [Philosophy of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_science "Philosophy of science") and [Sociology of scientific knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociology_of_scientific_knowledge "Sociology of scientific knowledge")
[the underpinning logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#polyaFirstUnderstand) of the scientific method, at what separates [science from non-science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demarcation_problem "Demarcation problem"), and the [ethic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_ethics "Research ethics") that is implicit in science. There are basic assumptions, derived from philosophy by at least one prominent scientist,[[D]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-introspection-199)[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-comprehensibility-200) that form the base of the scientific method – namely, that reality is objective and consistent, that humans have the capacity to perceive reality accurately, and that rational explanations exist for elements of the real world.[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-comprehensibility-200) These assumptions from [methodological naturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalism_\(philosophy\) "Naturalism \(philosophy\)") form a basis on which science may be grounded. [Logical positivist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logical_positivism "Logical positivism"), [empiricist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empiricism "Empiricism"), [falsificationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability "Falsifiability"), and other theories have criticized these assumptions and given alternative accounts of the logic of science, but each has also itself been criticized. 
There are several kinds of modern philosophical conceptualizations and attempts at definitions of the method of science.[[λ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-202) The one attempted by the _unificationists_ , who argue for the existence of a unified definition that is useful (or at least 'works' in every context of science). The _pluralists_ , arguing degrees of science being too fractured for a universal definition of its method to by useful. And those, who argue that the very attempt at definition is already detrimental to the free flow of ideas. 
Additionally, there have been views on the social framework in which science is done, and the impact of the sciences social environment on research. Also, there is 'scientific method' as popularised by Dewey in _How We Think_ (1910) and Karl Pearson in _Grammar of Science_ (1892), as used in fairly uncritical manner in education. 
### Pluralism
Main article: [Scientific pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_pluralism "Scientific pluralism")
Scientific pluralism is a position within the [philosophy of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_science "Philosophy of science") that rejects various proposed [unities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_of_science "Unity of science") of scientific method and subject matter. Scientific pluralists hold that science is not unified in one or more of the following ways: the [metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics "Metaphysics") of its subject matter, the [epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemology "Epistemology") of scientific knowledge, or the [research methods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_methods "Research methods") and models that should be used. Some pluralists believe that pluralism is necessary due to the nature of science. Others say that since [scientific disciplines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_discipline "Scientific discipline") already vary in practice, there is no reason to believe this variation is wrong until a specific unification is [empirically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirically "Empirically") proven. Finally, some hold that pluralism should be allowed for [normative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normative "Normative") reasons, even if unity were possible in theory. 
### Unificationism
Main article: [Unity of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_of_science "Unity of science")
Unificationism, in science, was a central tenet of [logical positivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logical_positivism "Logical positivism").[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Neurath%E2%80%A0_Bonk_2011-203)[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-McGill_1937-204) Different logical positivists construed this doctrine in several different ways, e.g. as a [reductionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reductionism "Reductionism") thesis, that the objects investigated by the [special sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_sciences "Special sciences") reduce to the objects of a common, putatively more basic domain of science, usually thought to be physics; as the thesis that all theories and results of the various sciences can or ought to be expressed in a common language or "universal slang"; or as the thesis that all the special sciences share a common scientific method.[[y]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-unifiedMethod-206)
Development of the idea has been troubled by accelerated advancement in technology that has opened up many new ways to look at the world. 
> The fact that the standards of scientific success shift with time does not only make the philosophy of science difficult; it also raises problems for the public understanding of science. We do not have a fixed scientific method to rally around and defend.
— [Steven Weinberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Weinberg "Steven Weinberg"), 1995[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Weinberg_1995-201)
### Epistemological anarchism
Main article: [Epistemological anarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemological_anarchism "Epistemological anarchism")
[Paul Feyerabend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Feyerabend "Paul Feyerabend") examined the history of science, and was led to deny that science is genuinely a methodological process. In his 1975 book _[Against Method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Against_Method "Against Method")_ he argued that no description of scientific method [could possibly be broad enough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#critiquesOfFeyerabend) to include all the approaches and methods used by scientists, and that there are no useful and exception-free [methodological rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodology "Methodology") governing the progress of science. In essence, he said that for any specific method or norm of science, one can find a historic episode where violating it has contributed to the progress of science. He jokingly suggested that, if believers in the scientific method wish to express a single universally valid rule, it should be '[anything goes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#theTermSci)'.[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-207) As has been argued before him however, this is uneconomic; [problem solvers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Problem_solving "Problem solving"), and researchers are to be prudent with their resources during their inquiry.[[E]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FRL-1.136-209)
A more general inference against formalised method has been found through research involving interviews with scientists regarding their conception of method. This research indicated that scientists frequently encounter difficulty in determining whether the available evidence supports their hypotheses. This reveals that there are no straightforward mappings between overarching methodological concepts and precise strategies to direct the conduct of research.[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Schickore_Hangel_2019-210)
### Education
See also: [Philosophy of education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_education "Philosophy of education") and [Scientific literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_literacy "Scientific literacy")
In [science education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_education "Science education"), the idea of a general and universal scientific method has been notably influential, and numerous studies (in the US) have shown that this framing of method often forms part of both students' and teachers' conception of science.[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Aikenhead_1987_pp._459%E2%80%93487-211)[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Osborne_Simon_Collins_2003_pp._1049%E2%80%931079-212) This convention of traditional education has been argued against by scientists, as there is a consensus that educations' sequential elements and unified view of scientific method do not reflect how scientists actually work.[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Bauer_1992_p.-213)[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-McComas_1996_pp._10%E2%80%9316-214)[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Wivagg_2002_pp._645%E2%80%93646-215) Major organizations of scientists such as the American Association for the Advancement of Science (AAAS) consider the sciences to be a part of the liberal arts traditions of learning and proper understating of science includes understanding of philosophy and history, not just science in isolation.[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-216)
How the sciences make knowledge has been taught in the context of "the" scientific method (singular) since the early 20th century. Various systems of education, including but not limited to the US, have taught the method of science as a process or procedure, structured as a definitive series of steps:[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-220) observation, hypothesis, prediction, experiment. 
This version of the method of science has been a long-established standard in primary and secondary education, as well as the biomedical sciences.[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-222) It has long been held to be an inaccurate idealisation of how some scientific inquiries are structured.[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Rudolph2005-217)
Traditional science education faced criticism for presenting an oversimplified, singular methodology that overemphasized experimentation, ignored social context, and suggested automatic knowledge generation through procedural steps.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Emden2021-223)[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-BrownKumar2013-224)
The scientific method no longer features in the standards for US education of 2013 ([NGSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Generation_Science_Standards "Next Generation Science Standards")) that replaced those of 1996 ([NRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Science_Education_Standards "National Science Education Standards")). They, too, influenced international science education,[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Emden2021-223) and the standards measured for have shifted since from the singular hypothesis-testing method to a broader conception of scientific methods.[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-IoannidouErduran2021-225) These scientific methods, which are rooted in scientific practices and not epistemology, are described as the 3 _dimensions_ of scientific and engineering practices, crosscutting concepts (interdisciplinary ideas), and disciplinary core ideas.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Emden2021-223)
The scientific method, as a result of simplified and universal explanations, is often held to have reached a kind of mythological status; as a tool for communication or, at best, an idealisation.[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Thurs2015-51)[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-McComas_1996_pp._10%E2%80%9316-214) Education's approach was heavily influenced by John Dewey's, _[How We Think](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_We_Think "How We Think") (1910)_.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-cowles-45) Van der Ploeg (2016) indicated that Dewey's views on education had long been used to further an idea of citizen education removed from "sound education", claiming that references to Dewey in such arguments were undue interpretations (of Dewey).[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-van_der_Ploeg_2016_pp._145%E2%80%93159-226)
### Sociology of knowledge
Main article: [Sociology of scientific knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociology_of_scientific_knowledge "Sociology of scientific knowledge")
The sociology of knowledge is a concept in the discussion around scientific method, claiming the underlying method of science to be sociological. King explains that sociology distinguishes here between the system of ideas that govern the sciences through an inner logic, and the social system in which those ideas arise.[[μ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-229)[[i]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Tanqih-21)
#### Thought collectives
A perhaps accessible lead into what is claimed is [Fleck's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwik_Fleck "Ludwik Fleck") thought, echoed in [Kuhn's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Kuhn "Thomas Kuhn") concept of [normal science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_science "Normal science"). According to Fleck, scientists' work is based on a thought-style, that cannot be rationally reconstructed. It gets instilled through the experience of learning, and science is then advanced based on a tradition of shared assumptions held by what he called [_thought collectives_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought_collective "Thought collective"). Fleck also claims this phenomenon to be largely invisible to members of the group.[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Fleck_comp_w/Kuhn-231)
Comparably, following the [field research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_research "Field research") in an academic scientific laboratory by [Latour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Latour "Bruno Latour") and [Woolgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Woolgar "Steve Woolgar"), [Karin Knorr Cetina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karin_Knorr_Cetina "Karin Knorr Cetina") has conducted a comparative study of two scientific fields (namely [high energy physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_physics "Particle physics") and [molecular biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_biology "Molecular biology")) to conclude that the epistemic practices and reasonings within both scientific communities are different enough to introduce the concept of "[epistemic cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemic_cultures "Epistemic cultures")", in contradiction with the idea that a so-called "scientific method" is unique and a unifying concept.[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-232)[[z]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-236)
#### Situated cognition and relativism
See also: [Postpositivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postpositivism "Postpositivism") and [Relativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativism "Relativism")
On the idea of Fleck's _thought collectives_ sociologists built the concept of [situated cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Situated_cognition "Situated cognition"): that the perspective of the researcher fundamentally affects their work; and, too, more radical views. 
[Norwood Russell Hanson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwood_Russell_Hanson "Norwood Russell Hanson"), alongside [Thomas Kuhn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Kuhn "Thomas Kuhn") and [Paul Feyerabend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Feyerabend "Paul Feyerabend"), extensively explored the theory-laden nature of observation in science. Hanson introduced the concept in 1958, emphasizing that observation is influenced by the [observer's conceptual framework](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Situated_cognition "Situated cognition"). He used the concept of [gestalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestalt_psychology "Gestalt psychology") to show how preconceptions can affect both observation and description, and illustrated this with examples like the initial rejection of [Golgi bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golgi_apparatus "Golgi apparatus") as an artefact of staining technique, and the differing interpretations of the same sunrise by Tycho Brahe and Johannes Kepler. [Intersubjectivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersubjectivity "Intersubjectivity") led to different conclusions.[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hanson1958-135)[[d]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Kepler1604-35)
Kuhn and Feyerabend acknowledged Hanson's pioneering work,[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-237)[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-238) although Feyerabend's views on methodological pluralism were more radical. Criticisms like those from Kuhn and Feyerabend prompted discussions leading to the development of the [strong programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strong_programme "Strong programme"), a sociological approach that seeks to explain scientific knowledge without recourse to the truth or validity of scientific theories. It examines how scientific beliefs are shaped by social factors such as power, ideology, and interests. 
The [postmodernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernism "Postmodernism") critiques of science have themselves been the subject of intense controversy. This ongoing debate, known as the [science wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_wars "Science wars"), is the result of conflicting values and assumptions between [postmodernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernist "Postmodernist") and [realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_realism "Scientific realism") perspectives. Postmodernists argue that scientific knowledge is merely a discourse, devoid of any claim to fundamental truth. In contrast, realists within the scientific community maintain that science uncovers real and fundamental truths about reality. Many books have been written by scientists which take on this problem and challenge the assertions of the postmodernists while defending science as a legitimate way of deriving truth.[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-239)
## Limits of method
### Role of chance in discovery
Main article: [Role of chance in scientific discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Role_of_chance_in_scientific_discoveries "Role of chance in scientific discoveries")
[![left](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Sample_of_penicillin_mould_presented_by_Alexander_Fleming_to_Douglas_Macleod%2C_1935_%289672239344%29.jpg/250px-Sample_of_penicillin_mould_presented_by_Alexander_Fleming_to_Douglas_Macleod%2C_1935_%289672239344%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sample_of_penicillin_mould_presented_by_Alexander_Fleming_to_Douglas_Macleod,_1935_\(9672239344\).jpg)A famous example of discovery being stumbled upon was Alexander Fleming's [discovery of penicillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Fleming#Discovery_of_penicillin "Alexander Fleming"). One of his bacteria cultures got contaminated with mould in which surroundings the bacteria had died off; thereby the method of discovery was simply knowing what to look out for.[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Tan_&_Tatsumura_2015-240)
Somewhere between 33% and 50% of all [scientific discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_discovery "Scientific discovery") are estimated to have been _stumbled upon_ , rather than sought out. This may explain why scientists so often express that they were lucky.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-DunbarLuck-9) Scientists themselves in the 19th and 20th century acknowledged the role of fortunate luck or serendipity in discoveries.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Serendip-10) [Louis Pasteur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Pasteur "Louis Pasteur") is credited with the famous saying that "Luck favours the prepared mind", but some psychologists have begun to study what it means to be 'prepared for luck' in the scientific context. Research is showing that scientists are taught various heuristics that tend to harness chance and the unexpected.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-DunbarLuck-9)[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Oliver,_J.E._1991-241) This is what [Nassim Nicholas Taleb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nassim_Nicholas_Taleb "Nassim Nicholas Taleb") calls "Anti-fragility"; while some systems of investigation are fragile in the face of [human error](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_error "Human error"), human bias, and randomness, the scientific method is more than resistant or tough – it actually benefits from such randomness in many ways (it is anti-fragile). Taleb believes that the more anti-fragile the system, the more it will flourish in the real world.[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Anti-fragility-242)
_think_ is an error in their method. Eventually, the researcher decides the error is too persistent and systematic to be a coincidence. The highly controlled, cautious, and curious aspects of the scientific method are thus what make it well suited for identifying such persistent systematic errors. At this point, the researcher will begin to think of theoretical explanations for the error, often seeking the help of colleagues across different domains of expertise.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-DunbarLuck-9)[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Oliver,_J.E._1991-241)
### Relationship with statistics
When the scientific method employs statistics as a key part of its arsenal, there are mathematical and practical issues that can have a deleterious effect on the reliability of the output of scientific methods. This is described in a popular 2005 scientific paper "[Why Most Published Research Findings Are False](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_Most_Published_Research_Findings_Are_False "Why Most Published Research Findings Are False")" by [John Ioannidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ioannidis "John Ioannidis"), which is considered foundational to the field of [metascience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metascience "Metascience").[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-mostRwrong-163) Much research in metascience seeks to identify poor use of statistics and improve its use, an example being the [misuse of p-values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misuse_of_p-values "Misuse of p-values").[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-243)
The points raised are both statistical and economical. Statistically, research findings are less likely to be true when studies are small and when there is significant flexibility in study design, definitions, outcomes, and analytical approaches. Economically, the reliability of findings decreases in fields with greater financial interests, biases, and a high level of competition among research teams. As a result, most research findings are considered false across various designs and scientific fields, particularly in modern biomedical research, which often operates in areas with very low pre- and post-study probabilities of yielding true findings. Nevertheless, despite these challenges, most new discoveries will continue to arise from hypothesis-generating research that begins with low or very low pre-study odds. This suggests that expanding the frontiers of knowledge will depend on investigating areas outside the mainstream, where the chances of success may initially appear slim.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-mostRwrong-163)
### Science of complex systems
Science applied to complex systems can involve elements such as [transdisciplinarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transdisciplinarity "Transdisciplinarity"), [systems theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systems_theory "Systems theory"), [control theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_theory#Open-loop_and_closed-loop_\(feedback\)_control "Control theory"), and [scientific modelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_modelling "Scientific modelling"). 
In general, the scientific method may be difficult to apply stringently to diverse, interconnected systems and large data sets. In particular, practices used within [Big data](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_data "Big data"), such as [predictive analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predictive_analytics "Predictive analytics"), may be considered to be at odds with the scientific method,[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-244) as some of the data may have been stripped of the parameters which might be material in alternative hypotheses for an explanation; thus the stripped data would only serve to support the [null hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Null_hypothesis "Null hypothesis") in the predictive analytics application. [Fleck (1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFFleck1979), pp. 38–50 notes "a [scientific discovery remains incomplete without considerations of the social practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#startWithBugs) that condition it".[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-bigDataCanBeIncomplete-245)
## Relationship with mathematics
Science is the process of gathering, comparing, and evaluating proposed models against [observables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observable "Observable"). _known_ from what is _unknown_ at each stage of discovery. Models, in both science and mathematics, need to be internally consistent and also ought to be _[falsifiable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiable "Falsifiable")_ (capable of disproof). In mathematics, a statement need not yet be proved; at such a stage, that statement would be called a [conjecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjecture "Conjecture").[[202]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-246)
Mathematical work and scientific work can inspire each other.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-ilSaggiatore-57) For example, the technical concept of [time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time "Time") arose in [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science "Science"), and timelessness was a hallmark of a mathematical topic. But today, the [Poincaré conjecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poincar%C3%A9_conjecture "Poincaré conjecture") has been proved using time as a mathematical concept in which objects can flow (see [Ricci flow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricci_flow "Ricci flow")).[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-247)
Nevertheless, the connection between mathematics and reality (and so science to the extent it describes reality) remains obscure. [Eugene Wigner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_Wigner "Eugene Wigner")'s paper, "[The Unreasonable Effectiveness of Mathematics in the Natural Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Unreasonable_Effectiveness_of_Mathematics_in_the_Natural_Sciences "The Unreasonable Effectiveness of Mathematics in the Natural Sciences")", is a very well-known account of the issue from a Nobel Prize-winning physicist. In fact, some observers (including some well-known mathematicians such as [Gregory Chaitin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Chaitin "Gregory Chaitin"), and others such as [Lakoff and Núñez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Where_Mathematics_Comes_From "Where Mathematics Comes From")) have suggested that mathematics is the result of practitioner bias and human limitation (including cultural ones), somewhat like the post-modernist view of science.[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-WMCF-248)
[George Pólya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_P%C3%B3lya "George Pólya")'s work on [problem solving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Problem_solving "Problem solving"),[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-findIt-249) the construction of mathematical [proofs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_proof "Mathematical proof"), and [heuristic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuristic "Heuristic")[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-250)[[207]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-251) show that the mathematical method and the scientific method differ in detail, while nevertheless resembling each other in using iterative or recursive steps. 
|  [Mathematical method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_to_Solve_It "How to Solve It") |  [Scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Elements_of_the_scientific_method)  
---|---|---  
1  |  [Understanding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Understanding "Understanding") |  [Characterization from experience and observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Characterizations)  
2  |  [Analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analysis "Analysis") |  [Hypothesis: a proposed explanation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Hypothesis_development)  
3  |  [Synthesis](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/synthesis "wikt:synthesis") |  [Deduction: prediction from the hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Predictions_from_the_hypothesis)  
4  |  [Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Review "Review")/[Extend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalization "Generalization") |  [Test and experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Experiments)  
_understanding_ involves restating unfamiliar definitions in your own words, resorting to geometrical figures, and questioning what we know and do not know already; _analysis_ , which Pólya takes from [Pappus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pappus_of_Alexandria "Pappus of Alexandria"),[[208]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEP%C3%B3lya1957142-252) involves free and heuristic construction of plausible arguments, [working backward from the goal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_backward_from_the_goal "Working backward from the goal"), and devising a plan for constructing the proof; _synthesis_ is the strict [Euclidean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclid "Euclid") exposition of step-by-step details[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEP%C3%B3lya1957144-253) of the proof; _review_ involves reconsidering and re-examining the result and the path taken to it. 
[Imre Lakatos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imre_Lakatos "Imre Lakatos") argued that mathematicians actually use contradiction, criticism, and revision as principles for improving their work.[[210]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-254)[[ν]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-stillwell'sReviewOfGray'sBioOfPoincar%C3%A9-255) In like manner to science, where truth is sought, but certainty is not found, in _[Proofs and Refutations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proofs_and_Refutations "Proofs and Refutations")_ , what Lakatos tried to establish was that no theorem of [informal mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informal_mathematics "Informal mathematics") is final or perfect. This means that, in non-axiomatic mathematics, we should not think that a theorem is ultimately true, only that no [counterexample](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterexample "Counterexample") has yet been found. Once a counterexample, i.e. an entity contradicting/not explained by the theorem is found, we adjust the theorem, possibly extending the domain of its validity. This is a continuous way our knowledge accumulates, through the logic and process of proofs and refutations. (However, if axioms are given for a branch of mathematics, this creates a logical system —Wittgenstein 1921 _Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus_ 5.13; Lakatos claimed that proofs from such a system were [tautological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tautology_\(logic\) "Tautology \(logic\)"), i.e. [internally logically true](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logical_truth "Logical truth"), by [rewriting forms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/String_rewriting_system "String rewriting system"), as shown by Poincaré, who demonstrated the technique of transforming tautologically true forms (viz. the [Euler characteristic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler_characteristic "Euler characteristic")) into or out of forms from [homology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homology_\(mathematics\) "Homology \(mathematics\)"),[[211]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-eulerPoincar%C3%A9-256) or more abstractly, from [homological algebra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homological_algebra "Homological algebra").[[212]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-257)[[213]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-258)[[ν]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-stillwell'sReviewOfGray'sBioOfPoincar%C3%A9-255)
Lakatos proposed an account of mathematical knowledge based on Polya's idea of [heuristics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuristic "Heuristic"). In _Proofs and Refutations_ , Lakatos gave several basic rules for finding proofs and counterexamples to conjectures. He thought that mathematical '[thought experiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought_experiment "Thought experiment")' are a valid way to discover mathematical conjectures and proofs.[[215]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos197655-260)
[Gauss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Friedrich_Gauss "Carl Friedrich Gauss"), when asked how he came about his [theorems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theorem "Theorem"), once replied "durch planmässiges Tattonieren" (through [systematic palpable experimentation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constructivism_\(mathematics\) "Constructivism \(mathematics\)")).[[216]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMackay1991100-261)

  * [Metascience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metascience "Metascience") – Scientific study of science
  * [Outline of scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outline_of_scientific_method "Outline of scientific method")
  * [Quantitative research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantitative_research "Quantitative research") – All procedures for the numerical representation of empirical facts
  * [Research transparency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_transparency "Research transparency")
  * [Scientific law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_law "Scientific law") – Statement based on repeated empirical observations that describes some natural phenomenon
  * [Scientific protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_protocol "Scientific protocol")
  * [Testability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testability "Testability") – Ability to examine a theory by experimentation


## Notes
  1. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-alhacenCharacterizes_16-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-alhacenCharacterizes_16-1) _[Book of Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Optics "Book of Optics")_ (_circa_ 1027) After anatomical investigation of the human eye, and an exhaustive study of human visual perception, Alhacen characterizes the first postulate of [Euclid's Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclid%27s_Optics "Euclid's Optics") as 'superfluous and useless' (Book I, [6.54] —thereby overturning Euclid's, Ptolemy's, and Galen's [emission theory of vision, using logic and deduction from experiment. He showed Euclid's first postulate of Optics to be hypothetical only, and fails to account for his experiments.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_theory_\(vision\) "Emission theory \(vision\)")), and deduces that light must enter the eye, in order for us to see. He describes the [camera obscura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camera_obscura "Camera obscura") as part of this investigation.
  2. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-straightLinesOnly_17-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-straightLinesOnly_17-1) _[Book of Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Optics "Book of Optics")_ Book Seven, Chapter Two [2.1] p.220: — light travels through transparent bodies, such as air, water, glass, transparent stones, in straight lines. "Indeed, this is observable by means of experiment".[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-smith2010-117)
  3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-33 "Jump up")** The full title translation is from [Voelkel (2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFVoelkel2001), p. 60.
  4. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Kepler1604_35-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Kepler1604_35-1) [_**c**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Kepler1604_35-2) Kepler was driven to this experiment after observing the partial solar eclipse at Graz, July 10, 1600. He used Tycho Brahe's method of observation, which was to project the image of the Sun on a piece of paper through a pinhole aperture, instead of looking directly at the Sun. He disagreed with Brahe's conclusion that total eclipses of the Sun were impossible because there were historical accounts of total eclipses. Instead, he deduced that the size of the aperture controls the sharpness of the projected image (the larger the aperture, the more accurate the image – this fact is now fundamental for optical system design). [Voelkel (2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFVoelkel2001), p. 61, notes that Kepler's 1604 experiments produced the first correct account of vision and the eye, because he realized he could not accurately write about astronomical observation by ignoring the eye. [Smith (2004)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFSmith2004), p. 192 recounts how Kepler used Giambattista della Porta's water-filled glass spheres to model the eye, and using an aperture to represent the entrance pupil of the eye, showed that the entire scene at the entrance pupil-focused on a single point of the rear of the glass sphere (representing the retina of the eye). This completed Kepler's investigation of the optical train, as it satisfied his application to astronomy.
  5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-particDev_41-0 "Jump up")** Sanches and Locke were both physicians. By his training in Rome and France, Sanches sought a method of science beyond that of the Scholastic Aristotelian school. Botanical gardens were added to the universities in Sanches' time to aid medical training before the 1600s. _See Locke[(1689) An Essay Concerning Human Understanding](https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/John_Locke#An_Essay_Concerning_Human_Understanding_\(1689\))_ Berkeley served as foil to the materialist System of the World of Newton; Berkeley emphasizes that scientist should seek 'reduction to regularity'.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-idealism-39) Atherton (ed.) 1999 selects Locke, Berkeley, and Hume as part of the empiricist school.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-40)
  6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-deweySchool_46-0 "Jump up")** On Dewey's Laboratory school in 1902: Cowles 2020 notes that Dewey regarded the Lab school as a collaboration between teachers and students. The five-step exposition was taken as mandatory, rather than descriptive. Dismayed by the Procrustean interpretation, Dewey attempted to tone down his five-step scheme by re-naming the steps to phases. The edit was ignored.
  7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-unification_60-0 "Jump up")** The topics of study, as expressed in the vocabulary of its scientists, are approached by a "single unified method".[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-cowles-45): pp.8, 13, 33–35, 60  The topics are [unified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_\(computer_science\) "Unification \(computer science\)") by its predicates, in a system of expressions. The unification process was formalized by [Jacques Herbrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Herbrand "Jacques Herbrand") in 1930.[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-herbrand-59)
  8. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-descartes_72-0 "Jump up")** "no opinion, however absurd and incredible, can be imagined, which has not been maintained by some of the philosophers". —Descartes[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-discourseOnMethod-71)
  9. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-leapIsInvolved_83-0 "Jump up")** "A leap is involved in all thinking" —John Dewey[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-82)
  10. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-93 "Jump up")** From the hypothesis, deduce valid forms using [modus ponens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning#Modus_ponens "Deductive reasoning"), or using [modus tollens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning#Modus_tollens "Deductive reasoning"). Avoid invalid forms such as [affirming the consequent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirming_the_consequent "Affirming the consequent").
  11. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-nextItemToSettle_102-0 "Jump up")** The goal shifts: after observing the x-ray diffraction pattern of DNA,[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-TeaTime-97)[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-photo51Explained-96) and as time was of the essence,[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-econ-99) Watson and Crick realize that fastest way to discover DNA's structure was not by mathematical analysis,[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-reasonsFirstRule-100) but by [building physical models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#DNA-iterations).[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-SameShape-101)

  14. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-128 "Jump up")** In _Two New Sciences_ , there are three 'reviewers': Simplicio, Sagredo, and Salviati, who serve as foil, antagonist, and protagonist. Galileo speaks for himself only briefly. But Einstein's 1905 papers were not peer-reviewed before their publication.
  15. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-142 "Jump up")** "What one does not in the least doubt one should not pretend to doubt; but a man should train himself to doubt," said Peirce in a brief intellectual autobiography.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-140) Peirce held that actual, genuine doubt originates externally, usually in surprise, but also that it is to be sought and cultivated, "provided only that it be the weighty and noble metal itself, and no counterfeit nor paper substitute".[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-141)
  16. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-155 "Jump up")** The philosophy of knowledge arising through observation is also called [inductivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductivism "Inductivism"). A radical proponent of this approach to knowledge was [John Stuart Mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill "John Stuart Mill") who took all knowledge – even mathematical knowledge – to arise from experience through induction. The inductivist approach is still common place, though Mill's extreme views are outdated today.[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Psillos_2013-154): 35 
  17. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Astronomy101_157-0 "Jump up")** [Hipparchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hipparchus "Hipparchus") used his own observations of the stars, as well as the observations by Chaldean and Babylonian astronomers to estimate Earth's precession.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-astron101-156)
  18. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-keplerNewton_159-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-keplerNewton_159-1) Isaac Newton (1727) [On the System of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophi%C3%A6_Naturalis_Principia_Mathematica#Book_3,_De_mundi_systemate "Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica") condensed Kepler's law of for the planetary motion of Mars, Galileo's law of falling bodies, the motion of the planets of the Solar system, etc. into consequences of his three laws of motion.[[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-systOfWorld-158) _See Motte's translation ([1846](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The_Mathematical_Principles_of_Natural_Philosophy_\(1846\)/The_System_of_the_World))_

Le Verrier (1859), (in French), ["Lettre de M. Le Verrier à M. Faye sur la théorie de Mercure et sur le mouvement du périhélie de cette planète"](https://archive.org/stream/comptesrendusheb49acad#page/378/mode/2up), Comptes rendus hebdomadaires des séances de l'Académie des sciences (Paris), vol. 49 (1859), pp.379–383.
