  21. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-164 "Jump up")** ... and [John Ioannidis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ioannidis "John Ioannidis"), in 2005,[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-mostRwrong-163) has shown that not everybody respects the principles of statistical analysis; whether they be the principles of inference or otherwise.
For broader coverage of this topic, see [§ Relationship with statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#Relationship_with_statistics).
  22. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-165 "Jump up")** For instance, extrapolating from a single scientific observation, such as "This experiment yielded these results, so it should apply broadly," exemplifies inductive wishful thinking. [Statistical generalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning#statistical_generalization "Inductive reasoning") is a form of inductive reasoning. Conversely, assuming that a specific outcome will occur based on general trends observed across multiple experiments, as in "Most experiments have shown this pattern, so it will likely occur in this case as well," illustrates faulty [deductive probability logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning#Probability_logic "Deductive reasoning").
  23. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-189 "Jump up")** Occam's razor, sometimes referred to as "ontological parsimony", is roughly stated as: Given a choice between two theories, the simplest is the best. This suggestion commonly is attributed to William of Ockham in the 14th-century, although it probably predates him.[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Baker-184)
  24. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-WeinertEddingtonEinstein_195-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-WeinertEddingtonEinstein_195-1) [Arthur Eddington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Eddington "Arthur Eddington"), 1920: "The relativity theory of physics reduces everything to relations; that is to say, it is structure, not material, which counts." — Weinert, giving the Einstein example and quoting: "Eddington, Space, Time and Gravitation (1920), 197"[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Weinert_2004-180)
  25. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-unifiedMethod_206-0 "Jump up")** The topics of study, as expressed in the vocabulary of its scientists, are approached by a "single unified method".[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-cowles-45): pp.8, 13, 33–35, 60  A topic is [unified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_theories_in_physics "Unification of theories in physics") by its [predicates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predicate_variable "Predicate variable"), which describe a [system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System "System") of mathematical [expressions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expression_\(mathematics\) "Expression \(mathematics\)").[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Knight1989-205): 93–94, 113–117  The values which a [predicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predicate_\(mathematical_logic\) "Predicate \(mathematical logic\)") might take, then serve as [witness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witness_\(mathematics\) "Witness \(mathematics\)") to the validity of a predicated expression (that is, _true_ or _false_ ; 'predicted but not yet observed'; 'corroborates', etc.).
  26. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-236 "Jump up")** Comparing 'epistemic cultures' with Fleck 1935, [Thought collectives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought_collective "Thought collective"), (_denkkollektiven_): _Entstehung und Entwicklung einer wissenschaftlichen Tatsache: Einfǖhrung in die Lehre vom Denkstil und Denkkollektiv_[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-233) [Fleck (1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFFleck1979), p. xxvii recognizes that [facts have lifetimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#genesisOfScientificFact), flourishing only after incubation periods. His selected question for investigation (1934) was "[HOW, THEN, DID THIS EMPIRICAL FACT ORIGINATE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought_collective#predicateIsNotStatement "Thought collective") AND IN WHAT DOES IT CONSIST?".[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-234) But by [Fleck 1979, p.27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#genesisOfScientificFact), the thought collectives within the respective fields will have to settle on common specialized terminology, publish their results and further intercommunicate with their colleagues using the common terminology, in order to progress.[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-235)
See also: [Cognitive revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_revolution "Cognitive revolution") and [Perceptual control theory § The methodology of modeling, and PCT as model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perceptual_control_theory#The_methodology_of_modeling,_and_PCT_as_model "Perceptual control theory")


### Notes: Problem-solving via scientific method
  1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-vacuum_15-0 "Jump up")** Twenty-three hundred years ago, Aristotle proposed that a [vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum "Vacuum") did not exist in nature; thirteen hundred years later, [Alhazen disproved Aristotle's hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#alhazen), using experiments on [refraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refraction "Refraction"),[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-treatiseOnLight2-13) thus deducing the existence of [outer space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_space "Outer space").[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-alhacenOnRefraction4.28-14)
  2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-20 "Jump up")** [Alhazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhazen "Alhazen") argued the importance of forming questions and subsequently testing them: "How does light travel through transparent bodies? Light travels through transparent bodies in straight lines only... We have explained this exhaustively in our _[Book of Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Optics "Book of Optics")_.[[b]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-straightLinesOnly-17) But let us now mention something to prove this convincingly: the fact that light travels in straight lines is clearly observed in the lights which enter into dark rooms through holes.... [T]he entering light will be clearly observable in the dust which fills the air.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-treatiseOnLight-18)
     * He demonstrated his conjecture that "light travels through transparent bodies in straight lines only" by placing a straight stick or a taut thread next to the light beam, as quoted in [Sambursky (1975)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFSambursky1975), p. 136 to prove that light travels in a straight line.
     * [David Hockney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hockney "David Hockney") cites Alhazen several times as the likely source for the portraiture technique using the [camera obscura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camera_obscura "Camera obscura"), which Hockney rediscovered with the aid of an optical suggestion from [Charles M. Falco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_M._Falco "Charles M. Falco"). _Kitab al-Manazir_ , which is Alhazen's _[Book of Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Optics "Book of Optics")_ , at that time denoted _Opticae Thesaurus, Alhazen Arabis_ , was translated from Arabic into Latin for European use as early as 1270. Hockney cites Friedrich Risner's 1572 Basle edition of _Opticae Thesaurus_. Hockney quotes Alhazen as the first clear description of the camera obscura.[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-truthSought4sake-19)
  3. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-aQuestion_67-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-aQuestion_67-1) [_**c**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-aQuestion_67-2) [_**d**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-aQuestion_67-3) In the [inquiry-based education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquiry-based_learning "Inquiry-based learning") paradigm, the stage of "characterization, observation, definition, ..." is more briefly summed up under the rubric of a Question. The question at some stage might be as basic as the [5Ws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5Ws "5Ws"), or _is this answer true?_ , or _who else might know this?_ , or _can I ask them?_ , and so forth. The questions of the inquirer spiral until the goal is reached.
  4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-introspection_199-0 "Jump up")** [Never fail to recognize an idea](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Page%3APopular_Science_Monthly_Volume_12.djvu/300)... .— C. S. Peirce, ILLUSTRATIONS OF THE LOGIC OF SCIENCE, SECOND PAPER. —HOW TO MAKE OUR IDEAS CLEAR. _Popular Science Monthly_ **Volume 12** , January 1878, p.286[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-How-85)
  5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-FRL-1.136_209-0 "Jump up")** [Peirce (1899)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFPeirce1899) First rule of logic (F.R.L)[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-reasonsFirstRule-100) Paragraph 1.136: From the first rule of logic, if we truly desire the goal of the inquiry we are not to waste our resources.[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-econ-99)[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-SuitableTest-172) — [Terence Tao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence_Tao "Terence Tao") wrote on the matter that not all approaches can be regarded as "equally suitable and deserving of equal resources" because such positions would "sap mathematics of its sense of direction and purpose".[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-taoTime-208)


  1. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Tanqih_21-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Tanqih_21-1) [Sabra (2007)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFSabra2007) recounts how [Kamāl al-Dīn al-Fārisī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kam%C4%81l_al-D%C4%ABn_al-F%C4%81ris%C4%AB "Kamāl al-Dīn al-Fārisī") came by his manuscript copy of [Alhacen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhacen "Alhacen")'s _[Book of Optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Optics "Book of Optics")_ , which by then was some two centuries old: al-Fārisī's project was to write an advanced optics treatise, but he could not understand optical [refraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refraction "Refraction") using his best resources. His mentor, [Qutb al-Din al-Shirazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutb_al-Din_al-Shirazi "Qutb al-Din al-Shirazi") recalled having seen Alhacen's manuscript as a youth, and arranged to get al-Fārisī a copy "from a distant country". al-Fārisī is now remembered for his Commentary on Alhacen's _Book of Optics_ in which he found a satisfactory explanation for the phenomenon of the [rainbow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow "Rainbow"): light rays from the sun are doubly refracted within the raindrops in the air, back to the observer.[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-230) Refraction of the colors from the sun's light then forms the spread of colors in the rainbow.


### Notes: Philosophical expressions of method
  1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-26 "Jump up")** His assertions in the __Opus Majus__ that "theories supplied by reason should be verified by sensory data, aided by instruments, and corroborated by trustworthy witnesses"[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-24) were (and still are) considered "one of the first important formulations of the scientific method on record".[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorlik2011\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidc_ShAgAAQBAJpgPA132_132\]-25)
  2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-empirical_37-0 "Jump up")** ...an experimental approach was advocated by Galileo in 1638 with the publication of _[Two New Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_New_Sciences "Two New Sciences")_.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGalileo_Galilei1638-36)
  3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-48 "Jump up")** Popper, in his 1963 publication of _Conjectures and Refutations_ argued that merely [Trial and Error](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_and_error "Trial and error") can stand to be called a 'universal method'.[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-trialAndErr-47)
  4. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-ethicalPosition_50-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-ethicalPosition_50-1) Lee Smolin, in his 2013 essay "There Is No Scientific Method",[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Smolin_2013-49) espouses two [ethical principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#ethicalPosition). Firstly: "we agree to tell the truth and we agree to be governed by rational argument from public evidence". And secondly, that ..."when the evidence is not sufficient to decide from rational argument, whether one point of view is right or another point of view is right, we agree to encourage competition and diversification". Thus echoing [Popper (1963)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFPopper1963), p. viii
  5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-feedTheMachinery_112-0 "Jump up")** The machinery of the mind can only transform knowledge, but never originate it, unless it be fed with facts of observation. —[C.S. Peirce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C.S._Peirce "C.S. Peirce")[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-How-85)
  6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-138 "Jump up")** "At the heart of science is an essential balance between two seemingly contradictory attitudes—an openness to new ideas, no matter how bizarre or counterintuitive, and the most ruthlessly skeptical scrutiny of all ideas, old and new. This is how deep truths are winnowed from deep nonsense." — [Carl Sagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Sagan "Carl Sagan")[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-137)
  7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-macKay_150-0 "Jump up")** The scientific method requires testing and validation [_a posteriori_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_evidence "Empirical evidence") before ideas are accepted.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-conjugatePairs-104)
  8. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-invariantTheme_182-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-invariantTheme_182-1) Friedel Weinert in _The Scientist as Philosopher_ (2004) noted the theme of invariance as a fundamental aspect of a scientific account of reality in many writings from around 1900 onward, such as works by [Henri Poincaré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Poincar%C3%A9 "Henri Poincaré") (1902), [Ernst Cassirer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Cassirer "Ernst Cassirer") (1920), [Max Born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Born "Max Born") (1949 and 1953), [Paul Dirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Dirac "Paul Dirac") (1958), [Olivier Costa de Beauregard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_Costa_de_Beauregard "Olivier Costa de Beauregard") (1966), [Eugene Wigner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_Wigner "Eugene Wigner") (1967), [Lawrence Sklar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Sklar "Lawrence Sklar") (1974), [Michael Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Friedman_\(philosopher\) "Michael Friedman \(philosopher\)") (1983), [John D. Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_D._Norton "John D. Norton") (1992), [Nicholas Maxwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Maxwell "Nicholas Maxwell") (1993), [Alan Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Cook_\(physicist\) "Alan Cook \(physicist\)") (1994), [Alistair Cameron Crombie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alistair_Cameron_Crombie "Alistair Cameron Crombie") (1994), [Margaret Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Morrison_\(philosopher\) "Margaret Morrison \(philosopher\)") (1995), [Richard Feynman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Feynman "Richard Feynman") (1997), [Robert Nozick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Nozick "Robert Nozick") (2001), and [Tim Maudlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Maudlin "Tim Maudlin") (2002).[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Weinert_2004-180) — [Deutsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Deutsch "David Deutsch") in a 2009 TED talk proclaimed that "the search for hard-to-vary explanations is the origin of all progress".[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-DD_TED-181)
  9. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-183 "Jump up")** Differing accounts of which elements constitute a _good theory_ : 
     * Kuhn (1977) identified: accuracy; consistency (both internal and with other relevant currently accepted theories); scope (its consequences should extend beyond the data it is required to explain); simplicity (organizing otherwise confused and isolated phenomena); fruitfulness (for further research);[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-178)
     * Colyvan (2001) listed simplicity/parsimony, unificatory/explanatory power, boldness/fruitfulness, and elegance;[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Colyvan-179)
     * Weinert (2004) noted the recurring theme of invariance;[[θ]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-invariantTheme-182)
     * [Hawking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Hawking "Stephen Hawking") (2010): simplicity/parsimony, unificatory/explanatory power, and elegance, but did not mention fruitfulness.[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hawking-143)
  10. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Pars_and_El_185-0 "Jump up")** ...Hawking & Mlodinow on criteria for a good theory: "The above criteria are obviously subjective. Elegance, for example, is not something easily measured, but it is highly prized among scientists." The idea of 'too baroque' is connected to 'simplicity': "a theory jammed with fudge factors is not very elegant. To paraphrase Einstein, a theory should be as simple as possible, but not simpler".[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Hawking-143) See also:[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Baker-184)
  11. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-202 "Jump up")** There is no universally agreed upon definition of the method of science. This was expressed with [Neurath's boat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurath%27s_boat "Neurath's boat") already in 1913. There is however a consensus that stating this somewhat nihilistic assertion without introduction and in too unexpected a fashion is counterproductive, confusing, and can even be damaging. There may never be one, too. As [Weinberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Weinberg "Steven Weinberg") described it in 1995:[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-Weinberg_1995-201)
> The fact that the standards of scientific success shift with time does not only make the philosophy of science difficult; it also raises problems for the public understanding of science. We do not have a fixed scientific method to rally around and defend.
  12. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-229 "Jump up")** "The sociology of knowledge is concerned with "the relationship between human thought and the social context in which it arises."[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-227) So, on this reading, the sociology of science may be taken to be considered with the analysis of the social context of scientific thought. But scientific thought, most sociologists concede, is distinguished from other modes of thought precisely by virtue of its immunity from social determination — insofar as it is governed by reason rather than by tradition, and insofar as it is rational it escapes determination by "non-logical" social forces." — M. D. King leading into his article on _Reason, tradition, and the progressiveness of science (1971)_[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-King_JA1971-228)
  13. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-stillwell'sReviewOfGray'sBioOfPoincar%C3%A9_255-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-stillwell'sReviewOfGray'sBioOfPoincar%C3%A9_255-1) Stillwell's review (p. 381) of Poincaré's efforts on the [Euler characteristic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euler_characteristic "Euler characteristic") notes that it took _five_ iterations for Poincaré to arrive at the _[Poincaré homology sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homology_sphere#Poincar%C3%A9_homology_sphere "Homology sphere")_.[[214]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_note-stillwell-259)

"There is a large core of people who think there is such a thing as a scientific method that can be justified, although not all agree as to what this might be. But there are also a growing number of people who think that there is no method to be justified. For some, the whole idea is yesteryear's debate, the continuation of which can be summed up as yet more of the proverbial 'flogging a dead horse'. We beg to differ. ... We shall claim that Feyerabend did endorse various scientific values, did accept rules of method (on a certain understanding of what these are), and did attempt to justify them using a meta methodology somewhat akin to the principle of [reflective equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reflective_equilibrium "Reflective equilibrium")."

The [Kamiokande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamiokande "Kamiokande") experiment, in Japan, independently observed [neutrinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino "Neutrino") from [SN 1987a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1987a "SN 1987a") at the same time.

  76. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-94 "Jump up")** In March 1917, the [Royal Astronomical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Astronomical_Society "Royal Astronomical Society") announced that on May 29, 1919, the occasion of a [total eclipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_eclipse "Total eclipse") of the sun would afford favorable conditions for testing Einstein's [General theory of relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_theory_of_relativity "General theory of relativity"). One expedition, to [Sobral, Ceará](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobral,_Cear%C3%A1 "Sobral, Ceará"), [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazil "Brazil"), and Eddington's expedition to the island of [Principe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principe "Principe") yielded a set of photographs, which, when compared to photographs taken at [Sobral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobral,_Cear%C3%A1 "Sobral, Ceará") and at [Greenwich Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Observatory "Greenwich Observatory") showed that the deviation of light was measured to be 1.69 [arc-seconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc-second "Arc-second"), as compared to Einstein's desk prediction of 1.75 [arc-seconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc-second "Arc-second"). – Antonina Vallentin (1954), _Einstein_ , as quoted by Samuel Rapport and Helen Wright (1965), _Physics_ , New York: Washington Square Press, pp. 294–295.

  78. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-photo51Explained_96-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-photo51Explained_96-1) [Cynthia Wolberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Wolberger "Cynthia Wolberger") [(2021) Photograph 51 explained](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2tMuMRY1oDo)
  79. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-TeaTime_97-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-TeaTime_97-1) [McElheny (2004)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFMcElheny2004), p. 52: Friday, January 30, 1953. Tea time — Franklin confronts Watson and his paper – "Of course it [Pauling's pre-print] is wrong. DNA is not a helix." However, Watson then visits Wilkins' office, sees [photo 51](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photo_51 "Photo 51"), and immediately recognizes the diffraction pattern of a helical structure. But additional questions remained, requiring additional iterations of their research. For example, the number of strands in the backbone of the helix (Crick suspected 2 strands, but cautioned Watson to examine that more critically), the location of the base pairs (inside the backbone or outside the backbone), etc. One key point was that they realized that the quickest way to reach a result was not to continue a mathematical analysis, but to build a physical model. Later that evening — Watson urges Wilkins to begin model-building immediately. But Wilkins agrees to do so only after Franklin's departure.
  80. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-Watson_1968_167_98-0 "Jump up")** [Watson (1968)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFWatson1968), p. 167: "The instant I saw the picture my mouth fell open and my pulse began to race." Page 168 shows the X-shaped pattern of the B-form of [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA "DNA"), clearly indicating crucial details of its helical structure to Watson and Crick.
  81. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-econ_99-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-econ_99-1) [_**c**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-econ_99-2) Peirce, Charles S. (1902), Carnegie application, see MS L75.329330, from [Draft D](http://www.cspeirce.com/menu/library/bycsp/l75/ver1/l75v1-08.htm#m27) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20110524021101/http://www.cspeirce.com/menu/library/bycsp/l75/ver1/l75v1-08.htm#m27) 2011-05-24 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine "Wayback Machine") of Memoir 27: "Consequently, to discover is simply to expedite an event that would occur sooner or later, if we had not troubled ourselves to make the discovery. Consequently, the art of discovery is purely a question of economics. The economics of research is, so far as logic is concerned, the leading doctrine concerning the art of discovery. Consequently, the conduct of abduction, which is chiefly a question of heuretic and is the first question of heuretic, is to be governed by economical considerations."

  85. ^ [Jump up to: _**a**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-conjugatePairs_104-0) [_**b**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-conjugatePairs_104-1) [_**c**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-conjugatePairs_104-2) [_**d**_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-conjugatePairs_104-3) MacKay, Donald M. (1969). _Information, Mechanism, and Meaning_. Cambridge, MA: MIT Press. pp. 1–4. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\) "ISBN \(identifier\)") [0-262-63032-X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-262-63032-X "Special:BookSources/0-262-63032-X"). "Invariably one came up against fundamental physical limits to the accuracy of measurement. ... The art of physical measurement seemed to be a matter of compromise, of choosing between reciprocally related uncertainties. ... Multiplying together the conjugate pairs of uncertainty limits mentioned, however, I found that they formed invariant products of not one but two distinct kinds. ... The first group of limits were calculable _a priori_ from a specification of the instrument. The second group could be calculated only _a posteriori_ from a specification of what was _done_ with the instrument. ... In the first case each unit [of information] would add one additional _dimension_ (conceptual category), whereas in the second each unit would add one additional _atomic fact_."

Bragg permitted them to restart their research on DNA (that is, model building).
  98. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-119 "Jump up")** [McElheny (2004)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFMcElheny2004), p. 54: Sunday, February 8, 1953 — Maurice Wilkes gave Watson and Crick permission to work on models, as Wilkes would not be building models until Franklin left DNA research.
  99. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-120 "Jump up")** [McElheny (2004)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFMcElheny2004), p. 56: [Jerry Donohue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Donohue "Jerry Donohue"), on sabbatical from Pauling's lab and visiting Cambridge, advises Watson that the textbook form of the base pairs was incorrect for DNA base pairs; rather, the keto form of the base pairs should be used instead. This form allowed the bases' hydrogen bonds to pair 'unlike' with 'unlike', rather than to pair 'like' with 'like', as Watson was inclined to model, based on the textbook statements. On February 27, 1953, Watson was convinced enough to make cardboard models of the nucleotides in their keto form.
  100. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-121 "Jump up")** [Watson (1968)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFWatson1968), pp. 194–197: "Suddenly I became aware that an [adenine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adenine "Adenine")-[thymine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thymine "Thymine") pair held together by two [hydrogen bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_bond "Hydrogen bond") was identical in shape to a [guanine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanine "Guanine")-[cytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytosine "Cytosine") pair held together by at least two hydrogen bonds. ..."
  101. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#cite_ref-122 "Jump up")** [McElheny (2004)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method#CITEREFMcElheny2004), p. 57: Saturday, February 28, 1953 — Watson tried 'like with like' and admitted these base pairs didn't have hydrogen bonds that line up. But after trying 'unlike with unlike', and getting [Jerry Donohue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Donohue "Jerry Donohue")'s approval, the base pairs turned out to be identical in shape (as Watson stated above in his 1968 _Double Helix_ memoir quoted above). Watson now felt confident enough to inform Crick. (Of course, 'unlike with unlike' increases the number of possible [codons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codon "Codon"), if this scheme were a [genetic code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_code "Genetic code").)

"In chapter six, Dewey analyzed what he called a "complete act of thought." Any such act, he wrote, consisted of the following five "logically distinct" steps: "(i) a felt difficulty; (ii) its location and definition; (iii) suggestion of possible solution; (iv) development by reasoning of the bearings of the suggestion; [and] (v) further observation and experiment leading to its acceptance or rejection.""

Schafersman.
